ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e201	The Council of State concentrated its presentation on five key challenges. It is important to remember that the most serious of these – the refugee and migration challenges and the weak economic growth following the financial crisis – are not created in Europe but outside. They are not the result of EU cooperation, but of war and the collapse of countries in the Middle East and Africa and the banking crackdown in the United States. On the contrary, we can wonder what the situation would have been like without EU cooperation, without the institutions and instruments that the European countries have through EU cooperation. We have seen the currents and forces under the pressure of the flood of refugees last year, because the countries together have not been able to get the Schengen and Dublin cooperation to deal with the difficult situation. Many countries turned their backs on common responsibility and instead took the matter into their own hands. We can only know what would become the situation if the option had not been to make these institutions work better, as it is now working on, and as the government supports. We agree that Norway has to do so. We are in both Schengen and Dublin and therefore also have a responsibility for this cooperation to work well, including in new and more demanding situations. What the EU is proposing is in line with our policies: border control, distribution between countries of those in need of protection, prevention of people spending their lives in the hands of traffickers who want to make money, not helping people. In the wake of economic problems, unemployment, migration and terrorist events, we see a worrying emergence of right-wing populist forces in several countries: the presidential elections in Austria between the candidates of the extremes, the populist and anti-democratic forces are strengthening – in Hungary, Poland, France and Russia. We have to stand up to these cancers. If we in Europe do not, how will we succeed in other parts of the world? I support the government that it is in our interest that the EU succeeds in developing its security and defence cooperation, and that Norway should be an active partner in this, not least in relation to Russia, as pointed out by the Council of State in its presentation. It must be central to us that our experience and our relationship with Russia are also involved in the European debate and analysis. We support the sanctions. However, it is also important to maintain areas for cooperation and dialogue, as we have in the north, on important common tasks: fisheries resource management, search and rescue, environmental management. This must be brought to the table by the government in cooperation with our neighbouring countries, both in the EU context, in NATO context and in Nordic-Baltic cooperation. Governments are concerned about the referendum in the United Kingdom. A majority to go out would create uncertainty. For us, it is important to bear in mind that in case they are not just leaving the EU, they are also leaving the EEA and therefore us. We will, once they have in that case snorted out of cooperation, also stand without a trade agreement with one of our main trading partners. It's worrying that the government doesn't seem to be preparing us for such a situation. The only answer from the Minister of Economic Affairs when I challenged her in a recent interpellation was that they were coming along. That's not good enough. I hope there are some members of the EFTA Secretariat in Brussels or Geneva who do more than just follow along. If the United Kingdom stays in the EU, and that is probably the most likely outcome, the Council of State says that the government sees an opportunity for increased flexibility in what Cameron has negotiated, in terms of the possibilities of reducing child welfare for children in countries with lower living costs than for us. Well, this has emerged from time to time in the debate over several years. However, it remains to be seen the thorough analyses of the extent of the problem of so-called social security exports — in addition to Norwegian pensioners who enjoy a warmer climate, whether in southern European countries or beyond, for example. in Thailand, and as there is an agreement to keep your pension, of course, as it is. There will be one side to purchasing power-adjusted child welfare that we cannot ignore – this will also apply to Norwegian children of Norwegian parents abroad, for example. diplomatic children, seafarers’ priests’ children and Norwegian employees of Norwegian enterprises abroad. That I think will dispel reactions. Governments are looking forward to zero lags in the implementation of new legislation after it has been incorporated into the EEA Agreement. It is good that the government's efforts in this area have succeeded – I would like to congratulate the Council of State on that result! We deserve the same legislation in the internal market in all countries, including ours. But from zero there's only one way to go. So I think the Council of State is aware that the pressure on the government colleagues has to stay up, unless the next measurement is going to be a slowdown. In the presentation, the Governments quoted the government platform: our European neighbours are close friends and important trading partners, and therefore Norwegian foreign policy must start in Europe. I would like to add: the EEA Agreement means that Norwegian domestic policies also start to a large extent in Europe. If implementation has been good in its own administration, 430 directives are in line to be included in the EEA Agreement. The reasons are different. Many are linked to the third energy market package, many to the financial supervision package, and several packages come – summer package, autumn package, winter package – in the energy union. In addition, there is agreement in the Storting that we should negotiate an agreement with the EU on common compliance with climate objectives. This means that we should deal with the legislation that the EU countries agree on, including in those sectors that are not subject to the quota obligation. In other words, we are facing quite extensive EU integration. This also raises the challenge that the Council of State did not address in its statement: how are we going to deal with the development of ever larger sets of horizontal policies? How are we going to solve the challenge of giving the EU competence, including fines, to businesses that do not comply with the rules of the agencies that are being set up? The second is that these packages often also contain policy objectives, as we do, for example. received in the Renewable Directive. Are the EEA countries to be included in these policy objectives, or only in the instruments? This is about the boundaries of the EEA Agreement and what I want to call integrity. One example of the issue is that we have now been dealt with on participation in the European Financial Supervisors. Finally, a solution was found by using ESA as a monitoring authority, in line with the two-pillar system of the EEA Agreement, a natural solution that it should have landed a long time ago. Should this be the model of other supervision, like Acer, and more coming? Or should this be dealt with on a case-by-case basis – time-consuming and more uncertain about the solutions? The last European Minister launched a long time ago the concept of ‘Europe Communication’, a more brief announcement on more comprehensive issues that the government would clarify with the Stortinget. Until now we have not seen the spin of such a message, and I have in all modesty proposed several good candidates. But the issues I point to here should at least be appropriate for such a message, so I challenge the Council of State on that. We will discuss the matter of financial supervision later, but let me only express in this context amazement that it seems to create a bit of drama because we are going to use § 115 in this case. That doesn't mean anything but that the transfer of sovereignty to ESA is something more than so-called little intervention. Otherwise, the matter is quite normal. We must follow the common rules for the financial industry in the internal market. Our own financial supervision is to oversee Norwegian financial institutions under this legislation, and ESA is to ensure that this is done – that is, a quite normal procedure. I believe that ESA expects a fairly affordable amount of work, because we have sound financial institutions, because we have sound financial supervision. So so far they've seen that they need to hire only one year's work for that job. The advantage of this solution is that the supervision of the financial industry throughout Europe is now strengthened and must follow the same rules. That should be reassuring. We have another important issue for consideration, and this is the agreement on a new period of EEA funding and the Norwegian Financial Mechanism – it is under discussion in the Foreign Affairs Committee and will be debated here later in the Chamber – and the fisheries agreement. In this respect, I would just like to remind you that a unanimous committee in the position on globalisation and trade pointed out that, although some improvement has been achieved in the forthcoming agreement, the market access of Norwegian fishing industry in Europe is unsatisfactory and would be threatened if there were a TTIP agreement between Europe and the EU. That's why it's necessary, I think, to start thinking as creatively as you can, to see if we can get better market access for an important sector.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e256	Let me first thank the Council of State for a very broad, thorough and good presentation. A Europe in continuous crisis response over the last year marked the debate on the EU/EEA statement six months ago. We can certainly say that we are still within what we can call a time of destiny for Europe – a Europe of instability. Last year's Paris terror was followed by bombs to the airport and metro in Brussels before Easter. Migration challenges weigh on several countries, and although inflows decreased over the winter, it now rises. There are vulnerable states in a belt around Europe, an aggressive and unpredictable neighbour in the east, Russia, as well as a number of other demanding conditions: high economic conversion needs, a growth that is waiting for itself, polarisation within the EU, lack of common positions and opposition to interest, emerging from extreme radical forces. One has what is described as a rebellion against the elite/the establishment, that Europe's small power is scattered from all sides, new protest parties are emerging in Europe, and there is uncertainty about political developments in the United Kingdom and the United States. Distances are no longer a guarantee of safety. The situation may still have a potential for significant political and institutional setback. What has been built, what has been achieved over the last decades, the common basis for rights and obligations that are binding on all EU members, the basis for freedom and growth in Europe – that is what this is all about. The only thing that is certain is that most things are uncertain and unpredictable, and that the rate of change is very fast. Migration challenges are a key keyword. They characterise all policy areas in individual countries and at EU level and have brought Schengen into a serious situation. A broad approach is needed to control inflows and external borders, with measures targeting countries of origin and transit, and to mitigate conflicts that generate refugee flows and mass migration, and to establish more effective measures in Europe. The agreement with Turkey on effective cooperation arrangements has been important in this way, and Germany’s leadership plays a very crucial role. The EU security strategy, which includes the fight against terrorism, organised crime, trafficking in human beings and online crime, must also be mentioned. During the EEA Council this week, we noticed that an important theme was on the agenda: the migration crisis, Russia, Ukraine and cooperation in the Arctic. And yesterday, the good news came that Greece gets about 10 billion. kr in emergency loans because they have delivered under the EU and IMF requirements for tightening up and reforming. I would like to take this opportunity to commend the government for its offensive European policy, based on a four-year overall European strategy, the annual work programme and coordination here at home through the Government's European Committee and, not least, the involvement of the Storting through frequent meetings of the European Parliament Committee. Work is systematically and proactively to be early in processes of importance to Norway. It is the interaction, coherence and synergies between what we do in Norway, in cooperation in the Nordic region, with the Baltic region and with the rest of Europe that will ensure Norway for the future. Europe’s foremost priority is its ability to cooperate in meeting common challenges. Yes, cooperation is the ‘only dangerous road’ that the government council of Aspaker so strikingly fixed in the nation on Tuesday. Compromise is policy at its best. Although solutions are not always equally popular in all countries, international cooperation is about giving and taking. Norway has been able to contribute to joint solutions, in close cooperation with the EU institutions and the Member States. The internal market is the bottom line of European cooperation and Norway’s partnership with the EU, and it is more important than ever in the light of the challenge picture. However, the relationship with Europe is equally important as a community of values: the importance of the EU and the EEA Agreement for Norway, for the creation of value added and well-being at home, cannot be overestimated. 75-80 pst. of our exports go to the EU, and the agreement gives us duty-free market access to 31 countries/500 million people. In an area of energy security, Norway now plays an important role for the EU as the largest supplier of gas to the EU market. Russia has been a major supplier, a monopolist, and, in addition to the economic burden it has imposed on some countries, at artificially high prices, it has also been a not insignificant element of political control. Now that Norway is relieving Russia as the largest supplier of gas to the EU, it offers new opportunities for several countries. It is an important strategic choice for many of the eastern European countries to bring their dependence on Russia to what can be called the scrappowder of history. Poland's President, Duda, just underlined that during his Norway visit this week. The EEA Agreement is about something more. It is not only about market access, but also about cooperation on innovation and research, creating new products, strengthening jobs – thus facilitating the transition to the Norwegian economy. The size shall, in a short time, deal with the consent proposal on the EEA financial instruments. It is Norway's basic position that we should contribute to economic and social cohesion in Europe. It also serves Norwegian interests. Norway’s contribution over the year is now referred to as voice by the European Parliament. New financial instruments for the period 2014-2021 were finally signed on 3 May of this year, following a fairly long procedure on the EU side. The term of the agreement is extended to seven years and has a total framework of 26,6 billion. kr for the EFTA/EEA countries. Norway finances all of this materially. Depending on the currency situation, Norway’s annual contribution will be about 3.4 billion. kr. The negotiations on market access for fish and fish products have been conducted in parallel and it has been made clear from the Norwegian side that agreement on the EEA contributions provided for a satisfactory solution in this area for Norway. It is essential that Norway establish a more strategic use of EEA funds. Through the focus on research, innovation, education, climate, green economy and measures in the field of migration, we will be able to contribute to increased security and security and to strengthen the EU’s competitiveness and growth – a growth estimated at about 1.8 pst. this year. Two separate funds totaling 100 million. the euro has been established reserved for youth employment and regional cooperation projects. Own resources have been set up to strengthen bilateral cooperation between Norway and the recipient countries. ‘Bremain, or not to Remain, that’s the question. What will this have to do with trade, community of values, stability and Norwegian interests? The United Kingdom is an important partner and one of our most important allies. It's about long historical lines. We got our first trade deal with the British in the 1200s, on fish. We share many values and views relating to economic issues and the internal market. The United Kingdom also plays an important role in foreign and security policy in Europe with its resources. 23 June is therefore an important day for Norwegian interests. Let's hope for continued British EU membership after June 23. It will be in the interests of the European Union and Norway. In securing Norway for the future and securing Norway’s interests, Europe and the EU are an essential part of our security policy and value anchoring. Norway and the EU share a common history and culture — and not least we share a common value base: democracy, human rights and the rule of law. Norway shall contribute jointly. We're part of a solution. We must avoid the Europe of national egoism and, as the Minister for Foreign Affairs described it in a previous statement, we are replacing walls with quickly. Norway depends on Europe, we have to stick with our most important friends and allies. Europe's challenges are our challenges. Lack of management of the situation in Europe will have consequences for Norway. A weak and fragmented EU will weaken Europe's security. A strong and united Europe, on the other hand, will also stabilise the rest of the world.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e296	I would first like to thank the Council of State for a thorough and very good presentation. She focuses on issues that concern the whole of Europe at the moment, so it can be said that the presentation is very topical. Mr Elisabeth Aspaker, Minister for the EEA and EU, began his half-yearly presentation to the European Parliament on 19 May on important EEA and EU issues with a quote from the Government's political platform: ‘European countries are Norway’s close neighbours, friends and main trading partners. Therefore, Norwegian foreign policy must begin in Europe.’ I very much agree with that. Europe and European cooperation face many challenges, and the Council of State focused on five of them that are particularly central. It is the refugee and migration challenges, a more demanding security policy situation for Europe, the future relations of the United Kingdom with the EU, the need for more sustainable growth, migration and global competitiveness and climate and energy. I don't intend to address all these challenges – this was very well explained by the Council of State, but I want to point out what is at least a point of light, and something we all, I think, have been waiting for a long time. Finally, we have reached the goal of negotiations with the European Union on new financial instruments and increased market access for fish and fish products. Negotiations were initiated in January 2014. Earlier this month, the government and the EU signed the agreements on new Norwegian contributions to the less prosperous countries of the EEA and the EU. Norway will contribute about 3.3 billion. So it's NOK 391 million. EUR, each year in the period 2014 to 2021. This is now for ratification in Stortinget – finally. It is a little strange that Norwegian media do not mention this much more. This is a lot of money, and it's a positive thing. EEA funds will contribute to a greener and more competitive Europe. Two of the main challenges facing Europe are likely to contribute to sustainable growth, change and global competitiveness and, hopefully, will contribute to more innovation in climate and energy. In the coming years, the EEA funds will focus primarily on stimulating innovation and growth in Europe based on cooperation between Norway and the beneficiary countries. I think it is very good that the government will give priority to using EEA funds for innovation, research and education. In addition, the government wants to give priority to energy security and to extend police and judicial cooperation between Norway and the recipient countries. That's good. The EEA funds have undoubtedly contributed to strengthening links and cooperation between Norway and the beneficiary countries. Further development of bilateral cooperation shall be emphasised in this new period. It will now be made possible, for example, to: Norwegian enterprises, researchers, municipalities, social partners and non-governmental organisations may continue to participate in programmes and cooperation projects. It will provide Norwegian players with international experience and access to important networks in Europe, which is our largest export market. Since the programming period for the financial instruments 2014-2021 applies from 1 May 2014 to 30 April 2021, I hope that the agreements will enter into force as soon as possible and that, when they are now being dealt with by the Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence Policy, they will, of course, be dealt with promptly there. There will be a new debate later. The programme period for the new contributions has increased from five to seven years. As the negotiations extended far into a new contractual period, a period of seven years ensures a more sound development and implementation of the programmes of the individual beneficiary countries. The objective of both of the new arrangements is, as for previous schemes, to contribute to the reduction of economic and social differences in the EEA and to the promotion of bilateral relations. In the negotiations, it has been very important that the experience gained during the previous period be used as a basis for the new arrangements. At the same time, the impact of the financial crisis has been considered in 2008. It has contributed to increased focus on growth and employment, including inclusive growth and sustainable growth. Sustainable and inclusive growth and employment will be a key priority for the EEA financial instruments in the coming period. In that case, particular attention is paid to what I said: innovation, research and education, especially in relation to energy and the environment. I think these are good measures for what the minister started with: ‘European countries are Norway’s close neighbours, friends and main trading partners. Therefore, Norwegian foreign policy must begin in Europe.’ That's what this actually is. The beneficiary countries will be the same as in the previous period, with the exception of Spain, which was phased out, but with the addition of Croatia, which has become an EU member. The exciting thing here is that cooperation with third countries, bordering on the countries of destination, such as the United Kingdom, is now being opened up. Ukraine and Moldova. It offers great opportunities for Norwegian actors and in many ways also other actors in the voluntary sector. Unfortunately, Norway did not get through its demand to eliminate a number of insignificant tariffs, which would have simplified the trade regime between the EU and Norway. The EU considered this to be outside the framework of the negotiations. However, the agreement is considered to be a significant improvement in market access to the EU. Only if it is seven years old also means more predictability of the industry and improves the possibility of exploiting duty-free import quotas. I cannot get enough emphasis on the importance of the Norwegian industry and the population of Norway, and the respective recipient countries, becoming well aware of this scheme. Here, when this comes into place, I think that much better information and marketing measures need to be taken on EEA funds. But there's going to be a debate about this later, I think, so then it'll probably be a little bit more about it. So over to a case that takes me, which might not be that positive. It is a directive I hope Norway can oppose if it remains in its present form. On 18 November 2015, the European Commission proposed amendments to the EU Arms Directive on the acquisition and possession of firearms. These proposals are justified by the increased terrorist threat and by the fact that several terrorist acts involving firearms have been committed. If the EU Arms Directive is adopted in its current form, it will undoubtedly affect the law-abiding hunting and shooting environment in Norway. It is totally wrong to want a safer country, given the fear of terrorism and the use of weapons. It makes sense for measures to prevent terrorism and armed crime, but the EU directive goes far too far, and many of the measures will primarily affect law-abiding hunters and sportsmen in this country. I, and hopefully the Progress Party, believe that we already have a strict and balanced arms law in Norway. All owners of legal weapons have been allowed by the police and are engaged in hunting or shooting competition. These are activities that have long and good traditions in Norway. It is a very large sports organization with several hundred thousand members through the Voluntary Skyttervesen, the Norwegian Shooting Association and the Norwegian Hunters' and Fishers' Association. If weapons legislation is to be amended, this must be decided by Norwegian politicians who know Norwegian conditions. The Party cannot accept any changes which in practice would prohibit almost all types of semi-automatic weapons. Such a ban will in practice put an end to gun shooting and organised shooting through the Sports Association. The same applies to the proposal for a ban on the acquisition of weapons before the age of 18, which in reality affects only top shooters. These are deprived of the possibility of participating in international competitions where they currently have all the rights and international arms passports. The intrusions that deal with hunting weapons have no proven effect on the fight against terrorism. I have understood that there has been more input from EU Member States, which also responds to this proposal. So I have a hope that this will change in Norway's favour when the negotiations are concluded, and that agreement will be reached by the Justice and Home Affairs Ministers in the next month, as well as by the European Parliament. Otherwise, I would like to conclude by warning you that Norway is going to have great opinions on what Britain is going to do in its important debate and referendum on its relationship with the EU, and not to conclude by what is best for Norway.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e370	I would also like to thank the Minister for his statement on the status of many issues relating to Norway's relationship with the EU. Cooperation in Europe is important, but the EU is unfortunately the wrong response to the challenges we face. It clearly shows the European people by the different choices that have been made in recent years. EU funding schemes are signed after a long process in the EU. One would think it would be faster when the gift package from Norway is so big. The growth is staggering, and only the bureaucracy to distribute the money is more than 80 million. kr in year. That it was the Progress Party in the government that would stand for such a use of money, no one had believed that their rhetoric was possible before they went into the government offices. From a four-word point of view, growth in bureaucracy has been a goal in itself. The centre party believes the extent of EEA funding has come out of all proportions. We are also not overconfident that the funds are used in the best way possible. The center game is to help those who need it the most. We can do that without going about a superbureaucracy in Brussels. The EU and EEA ministers stressed the importance of Norway’s implementation in the new agreement on fish exports to the EU and that it provides significantly better access to the market. The truth is that the new lights at customs are of marginal value only for Norway. Nevertheless, there have been extensive internal procedures in the EU, which have delayed much of the entry into the agreement. The fact that one has made a redeployment of unexploited free allowances against obtaining new quotas for fillet production of mackerel and herring products is positive, but not decisive. If this would have an effect at all, it would not depend on the EU's quotas, but mainly on whether the high Norwegian level of labour costs would prevent processing that has moved abroad due to the level of costs in Norway from moving back home. Nor has it been agreed at this time on permanent quota arrangements that go beyond Norway already have the Treaty, either through the Free Trade Agreement of 1973, the EEA Agreement or the WTO. The strong EU export growth, both in terms of quantity and value, has failed in all predictions for more than 25 years that customs duties into the EU would have a strong trade dampening effect. All such prophecies are made to be ashamed. The main problem of finished product production in Norway has been high labour costs, much higher than in China, Poland and Latvia. Zero duties have little impact on product groups which already have almost zero customs duties, such as: finished production – fillet, panet, etc. of cod, hay and seine. Neither Frionor nor Findus downloaded due to customs duties; they failed to meet high rates of customs duties. It is a pity that fiction about customs barriers should govern Norwegian foreign policy with regard to trade with the EU. The fact that Norway has a number of duty-free quotas that Norway has now replaced speaks its clear language. Current EU customs duties do not represent a decisive barrier to trade. In the nations we can read that the Article 19 negotiations are going on. Wherever the bulwark leads us, the council won't say anything, so there's little we can say about it. However, I would like to recall the importance of the percentage of customs duties and other measures to provide room for Norwegian food production within the WTO Agreement and other obligations of Norway. I want to warn the government not to tangle with stakes at the Norwegian country-based food production. The majority of the population has clearly said that we should increase food production. The government has to follow it up. In these days, the Financial Committee is in a hurry to deal with the greatest renunciation of sovereignty since the EEA Agreement, where the majority will link Norway to EU financial supervision. The case gives ESA and the EFTA Court a new image in the financial sector. They shall take binding decisions for the Norwegian Governing Council and financial institutions and impose fines. However, the real content of the matter is that Norway is placed under EU financial supervision. ESA becomes a pure copy office. The European Agency shall supervise and take decisions, which shall then be copied via ESA and sent to Norway. Follow-up to the proposal shall only be assigned to one person in ESA to resolve the so-called supervisory task. In addition, a large and robust copier must be purchased. The financial crisis showed us that Norway has a well-functioning financial supervision – far better than its EU, even though it has come after the last few years. We quickly got on the feet after the financial crisis. In the EU, the euro crisis is a continuing obstacle to growth and development. Close ties and considerable impact from the financial industry on EU legislation have been demonstrated, and a playful document from negotiations between the EU and the United States on the TTIP agreement shows that the EU Commission is most concerned with resolving so-called barriers to trade and ensuring capital flow rather than regulation. So why link Norway to EU financial supervision if you want to ensure the proper functioning of supervision of Norwegian banking and financial corporations? Of course, because the majority of population has one, and only one, goal for the eye, simply integrating Norway as much as possible into the EU. Yesterday, we were told that the Minister of Finance was standing in Stortinget and that it is not a renunciation of sovereignty to link Norway to EU financial supervision. Do we relinquish sovereignty to the EU when we link ourselves to EU financial supervision? Or is that what the Minister of Finance said yesterday?

ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e585	Here in Norway, we are in a transformation of the Norwegian economy. In a small open economy, as we in Norway are, we are totally dependent on global rules in the field of trade policy, and we need a well-functioning market for international trade. The low oil price is demanding for many companies, and the consequences for many are reduced activity and cancellations of additions. Here in Norway we have more tonnage than the sailors may need, and therefore we need access to others' continental soles, others' waters, others' goods, and others' ports. It is in our interest that the EU should be as united as possible and that Europe should take greater responsibility for its own security. Together, the EU and NATO have a wide range of instruments. There is no contradiction between European and Atlantic orientation in Norwegian foreign policy, on the contrary. Many today demand a common vision of how the EU can develop further. Some countries want closer integration, while others focus on national solutions. The choices now being made by the Member States will characterise the EU’s development for a long time in the future. Norway is part of this European destiny. The terrorist attack on youth in Paris last autumn was another meaningless attack on our common values, where we too were hit. Therefore, Norwegian foreign policy begins in Europe. It is in Norwegian interest that the EU succeeds in developing its security and defence cooperation. Without the United Kingdom, the EU will lose its second most populous country and its second largest economy. According to the OECD, a discharge could reduce the United Kingdom’s GDP by 3.3 pst. by 2020, while the loss for the other EU countries is estimated at around 1 pst. GDP. Norway and the United Kingdom share many views on economic issues and the internal market. The United Kingdom is an important driver of the development of the internal market, which is also an advantage for Norway. With Europe’s major global competitiveness challenges, there is a need for actors such as the United Kingdom, who are pushing for economic reform in the EU. The United Kingdom is a significant defence power and an important player in foreign and security policy, both in Europe and globally. It is therefore important for the development and direction of EU foreign and security policy and for EU cooperation with NATO, not least. I am pleased that the EU/EEA Minister is actively working to safeguard Norwegian interests in the wake of the British referendum. Regardless of the outcome, the United Kingdom will remain a close partner and ally for Norway in the future. Norway contributes heavily in Europe. Europe's challenges are our challenges.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e698	Norway is dependent on close political and economic cooperation with the EU. Norway has chosen to interact with the EU through different contractual relationships, where the EEA Agreement is the most important and extensive. The EEA Agreement gives us access to the EU's internal market. As part of the EEA Agreement, we also need to have a link with the EU system of financial supervision. Following the financial crisis, which showed several gaps in financial market regulation and the failure of several national supervisory authorities, it has been important to put in place a single European system to ensure a more stable and well-functioning financial market in order to prevent new crises. From EU financial supervision in 2011, Norway has been working to reach a good agreement, and in 2014 there was a political consensus on a model. Since then, work has continued at the level of office until the solution that the government is now presenting to the Stortinget. By incorporating the necessary legal acts establishing the European System of Financial Supervisors, a Norwegian economic operator provides them with the same conditions and the same protection as EU countries in the internal market. The inclusion will facilitate the participation of the Norwegian Financial Supervisory Authority in cooperation with the financial supervision of the EU Member State. The EEA adaptations imply that the authority to take decisions directly in Norway is added to the EFTA Surveillance Authority, with the right to review the EFTA Court. Parts of this transfer of authority are of such a nature that consent is required with three-quarters plural — with at least two-thirds of the representatives present, in accordance with the requirement of Grundlova § 115. There is limited authority to be transferred to the EFTA Surveillance Authority, ESA. These are the supervision of the credit rating agency and some types of registries for securities transactions, the right to prohibit or restrict certain types of financial activity or savings product, the right to decide between national supervision and the right to take decisions directly against market participants in the event that national supervision would not do its job. However, most of the supervision of the financial market and financial activities in Norway remains with the Financial Supervisory Authority. It is important that Norwegian financial industries remain part of the European market and that financial institutions can provide Norwegian consumers and Norwegian business with good and full value. Secures the currently negotiated agreements.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e804	First of all, I would like to thank the whole Committee for its very good cooperation in this matter, a matter that has been going on for over a year and a half. The case includes a comprehensive correspondence, and two major hearings were held, one on 30 April 2015 and one on 26 and 27 November 2015. This case concerns the sale of military equipment by the Defence Agency and is an initiative matter on the part of the Committee, the background of which is a series of articles in Dagbladet in 2014 on the conditions of sale of a number of former Norwegian maritime vessels alleged to have landed with paramilitary groups in Nigeria, and also later in other West African countries. The matter was initially quite large and complex in nature, and it gradually became somewhat larger, as the Committee's position on the Storting shows. This case is set for Stortinget in two settings. It's Innst. 213 S for 2015–2016, dated 15 March 2016, and additional position Innst. 213 S Appendix 1 for 2015-2016. The former suspension was first introduced to treatment in Stortinget on 26 April 2016. At the request of the Committee, the opinion was sent back to the Committee. The reason for the request was that on 22 April the Committee received new documentation from the Minister of Defence, as stated. Letter of 21 April 2016. The majority of the Committee, all with the exception of the members from the Right, refers to the large volume of the material transmitted, in which the Committee had in fact only one working day to examine the reports received prior to the examination in the Stortinget. The majority therefore consider that there is reason to criticise the fact that the reports received were too late for the Committee to be able to handle the matter properly in the Stortinget on 26 April. The documentation included two reports with annexes, respectively a report from the Ministry of Defence’s Internal Audit Office on the disposal of material, dated 23 November 2015 and a final report from the Ministry of Defence’s Internal Audit Office on the disposal of F-5 fighter aircraft, dated 11 April 2016. The Committee is broadly in agreement with its comments. However, at some points there are differences, which will be natural in such a large, complex and important case. I expect that the individual members themselves will explain their comments in more detail during their oral contributions today. It was journalist Kristoffer Egeberg who, in the edition of Dagbladet on 14 June 2014, entitled “Frequent Ex-Pirate Uses Norwegian Warships”, made the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee aware of the matter. The insight into the matter was as follows: ‘The daily newspaper can today reveal how the Norwegian authorities sold several marine vessels to a British safety company registered at a campsite in Surrey. Now the ships are part of a private-powered marine power owned by a former Nigerian rebel leader to hunt pirates and collect taxes for one of the world's most lawless and corrupt countries.» A series of articles in 2014 mentioning the so-called Nigeria boats contained a number of serious claims. In view of this, in October 2014, the Committee decided to send a letter to Minister of Defence Ine M. Eriksen Soreide and Minister of Foreign Affairs Børge Brende, asking questions about the facts of the case and whether legislation and guidelines had been followed by the departments and agencies. The boats mentioned in the first row by the newspaper's series of articles relate to missile torpedo boats, or to daily called MTBs, of the Hauk class. This was originally a fleet force consisting of 14 boats, built between 1978 and 80, which reviewed a major upgrade around 2000 for relatively large costs, in order to be able to serve until 2015. In addition, a major vessel, KNM ‘Horten’, was added, which was a supporting vessel to the MTBs and submarines. However, most of the MTBs were destroyed in 2008, when, despite the subsequent upgrade, it was decided to take them out of operational operation. Six boats were decided to be disposed of through a disposal order issued in January 2009. The series of articles of the newspaper pointed out that the defence had not checked carefully the company to which the ships were sold and that it appeared to be a breach of export legislation to sell such ships to private operators who could arm them and provide security services off the coast of Africa. The articles of the newspaper duly stated that it was difficult to obtain information from the Defence Logistics Organisation, the FLO, on the sale of the marine vessels. In the light of the information provided to the Committee through the media in this case, the Committee sought answers to: whether such sales are lawful or under the rules of disposal if those who made the sale knew what they were to be used for and by whom respectively the Defence/Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs are responsible for the liability within the Defence for the dialogue between the Defence and Ministry of Defence and, finally, for the openness and willingness to inform the outside world; The main task of the Control and Constitutional Committee has been to clarify how former military ships and other military materials could be ported, inter alia. in West Africa or countries which are subject to the arms embargo and whether the regulatory changes made in the light of the specific cases are considered sufficient to prevent similar issues in the future. The Committee would stress the importance of having a defence subject to democratic control. Under the applicable provisions when the vessels were exported, the vessels were subject to licensing. A concept was developed for the conversion and demilitarisation of vessels so that they were no longer subject to licensing obligations under the Ministry of Foreign Affairs' control lists. On 13 February 2012, the defence entered into a contract for the sale of the vessels to the UK company CAS Global. The firm’s end-user statement confirmed that the vessels were to operate under fisheries supervision in the Economic Community of West African States, or the ECOWAS region, which is designated daily, some of which was also subject to the arms embargo. The ships were to be registered under the flag of the United Kingdom, manned by European teams and under the jurisdiction of the United Kingdom. Displaying the vessels of representatives of the company CAS Global, including Nigerian citizens, was carried out and it was then publicly known that CAS Global also had offices outside the United Kingdom, including the United Kingdom. Nigeria. On 20 August 2012, the Defence Logistics Organisation (FLO) issued the Bill of Sale on the sale of the six MTBs to CAS Global. The MTBs were declared export to England on 22 April 2013. The first three boats were sailed to England by their own crew, while the last three were transported from Norway on a larger cargo ship. The Committee points out that for several of the ship's sales there have been some major shortcomings in the absence of any assessment of the "capacities" provision and lack of evidence of the sales process itself such as disposal protocols, purchase contracts, etc. In addition, the customer assessment guidelines have not been satisfactorily followed up. In retrospect, it is easy for the Committee to see that the English companies have only acted as straw companies for the purpose of obtaining approval for the sale and export of the vessels to Nigeria. The Committee refers to the information provided by the customs director during the hearing that during the period 2008-2014 the defence has disposed of 35 vessels, 21 of which have been exported directly to abroad. After what appears in the media, 13 of these vessels are located in Africa, 11 of them in Nigeria. In addition to the vessels mentioned above, the disposal of KV “Titran” and KV “Agder” has also been carried out very poorly in relation to the rules of disposal. For the part of KV, Titran, in particular, it is aspects of this disposal that have led to a police report, and I therefore do not comment on this matter here. The majority of the Committee, with the exception of the members of the Right, the Progressive Party and the Centre, considers it necessary to have clear implications for those responsible for the breach of the rules in this case. The members of the Committee of the Right, the Progressive Party and the Centre do not consider it to be the task of the Stortingen to impose specific human resources on the Minister of Defence in this matter, but assume that the Council of State itself considers the administrative measures that need to be taken to reduce the risk of recurrence of breaches of the Code. I expect that the parties behind the majority vote themselves will explain in more detail the background to their comment on this point. This case also includes the export of belt wagons to Sudan, the export of military vehicles to Eritrea and the export of belt wagons to Sweden. This part of the case complex deals primarily with the export and not the sale of military material by the defence. As regards the export of the military vehicles to Eritrea, the persons responsible have been required to provide a package of 3 million. kr. They have not adopted the order to bring this part to justice. In the case of carriages for belts to Sudan, the Committee has received confirmation from the Ministry of Foreign Affairs that the wagons are the ship out of Sudan after they have done the service they should for the company that was to use them for oil exploration. The case of coaches for Sweden has also been accepted by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, and the Committee is satisfied with this. The last part of the case complex that was dealt with by the committee was the sale of two pieces of F-5 fighter aircraft that were in the United States at the time of sale. In the spring of 2015, the Ministry of Defence received a tip from an external person about possible critical conditions related to the sale of these aircraft. On the basis of the preliminary investigations carried out, the Ministry of Defence's internal audit found the basis for the sale and export of the two F-5 fighter aircraft to be deficient. Based on the results of the feasibility study and the presentation by the Defence Staff, it was decided to carry out an extended investigation. This study concluded with: The defence has not been able to provide evidence that the buyer represents the US authorities. There is no valid export licence from the U.D. for the period from 1 October 2008 to 26 January 2015. It cannot be established whether the aeroplanes have been used in the United States without the necessary permits. The disposal process is so poorly documented that it is not possible to verify whether one has tried to obtain the best price for the aeroplanes and whether alternative disposal methods have been evaluated. Given the potential seriousness of the matter and the possibilities of maladministration, the Minister of Defence decided that the report should be submitted to Ecocrim at the same time as the report was sent to the Committee. During the hearing in Stortinget on 27 November 2015, criticism was raised that the report was not submitted to the Defence for Objection, or opinion, before it was forwarded to the Committee. The majority of the Committee points out that the Council of State had no other choice, since the Committee had expressly asked to be briefed on all similar matters. During the same hearing, both the head of defence and FLO were asked if the sale of the two F-5 fighter planes was to be considered a ‘green sale’, which they both confirmed positive. I want to refer here to what the head of defense said: ‘Yes, I can confirm that. I have here the entire sales protocol from that process, and the last part, with the sale itself, has been completed after the first issues came up. The very decision to sell to the NGL was probably just before the authority was withdrawn from the head of defence. But the authority is attached to the head of defense in the last part of the process, clarifying up against the US Department of State and up against the U.D. We have carefully reviewed this, and we also received a round from the Ministry of Defence with an extra check just after the sales were completed, to verify that the U.D. had received all the information from us. We did, and the U.D. confirmed it." The final report of the Ministry of Defence's internal audit on the sale of two F-5 fighter aircraft dated 11 April 2016, as mentioned at the outset, concludes that: The aircraft in the United States were under Norwegian control and were not disposed of in violation of Norwegian legislation. The aircrafts were sold to operators that had the necessary approval by both Norwegian and US authorities. The sale was approved by both the Ministry of Defence, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the US authorities. On the other hand, there are major defects in the sales process itself. There are no or insufficient sales protocols, so that there is low traceability/reprobability of the sales process. There are several examples that stakeholders have not been followed up, have not received the same information, and that stakeholders have been rejected without evidence of why. It is clear that the non-statutory principle of equality has not been followed in the process. From the report: ‘The disposal process is characterised by weak internal control. A risk of maladministration is mentioned, but there is no suspicion or evidence of maladministration, and thus does not constitute a basis for the notification of potentially criminal offences. For FD Led IR’ – Ministry of Defence’s internal control – ‘it appears unclear what key controls the defence has actually taken into account in this sales process.’ The Committee points out that for five out of seven sales contracts there are no protocols and for the two existing protocols there are significant weaknesses. There are further indications that the defence has favourably treated the NGL. The majority of the Committee, all with the exception of the Right Party and the Centre Party, is surprised at the conclusion of the report that both the head of defence and the head of the FLO in the hearing on 27 November 2015 could declare the sale of the F-5 fighter aircraft ‘green’ when there are so many defects in the sales process itself. In a letter dated 29 April 2016, the Minister of Defence briefed that the final report of the internal audit had been sent to Ecokrim, as was the preliminary report. This just shows the seriousness of the matter. The Committee has noted that, by letter to the Ministry of Defence, Økokrim has informed that they do not currently have an investigation aimed at the disposal of F-5 fighter aircraft and that the updated final report of the Ministry of Defence's internal audit is being briefed and will not be subject to further follow-up by them. The majority of the Committee, the members of the Workers' Party, the Christian People's Party, the Left, the Socialist Left Party and the Green Party, refer to the final report on the sale of F-5 fighter aircraft and to the shortcomings identified by the internal audit and, in this context, will make the following proposals: ‘The report requests the Riksrevision to review the entire sales process concerning the sale of the two F-5 fighter aircraft mentioned in the final report of FD Led IR, dated 11 April 2016.’ The member of the Committee from the Central Party refers to the final report on the sale of F-5 fighter aircraft and points out that the final report states that the role of the Ministry of Defence is limited. The member of the Central Party joins the criticism of the internal audit that the formalities in the sales were not in order, and believes that the head of defence's organisation did too bad a job and finds this very serious. The member of the Central Party of the Committee has, in this context, made the following proposals: ‘The State Auditor’s report requests the National Auditor’s review of the entire sales process, including the role of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Ministry of Defence, on the sale of the two F-5 fighter aircraft mentioned in the final report of FD Led IR, dated 11 April 2016.’ The Committee further refers to the report of the Ministry of Defence's Internal Audit on the disposal of material dated 23 November 2015, which shows that the report has resulted in 24 recommendations that could improve internal control. The overview attached to the report shows that 13 of the proposals have been implemented and the remaining 11 are being implemented. The Committee welcomes this. In September 2014, the Export Control Code was tightened up by the introduction of licensing obligations for all materials that have been used for military purposes and that have at some time been covered by the Export Control Regulation List I material — this regardless of the state of the material on export. These changes have led to the Norwegian legislation now being equivalent to the English one, so that circumvention of the rules by using Norway as a transit country is no longer possible, as we have unfortunately seen examples of by way of example. the export of trucks to Eritrea. In these cases, the Committee would commend the customs services for addressing these types of cases and for asking questions about the legality of the export of the respective material. In these cases, the Customs Authority has shown good judgment by contributing to the sale/export of former military material under the provisions in force at all times. In addition to legislative changes, the Committee is satisfied with the measures taken by the Defence Agency to prevent new cases. However, the majority of the Committee considers that the head of defence has not been sufficient for the desire of the Council of State and the Grand Duchy to defend a defence which takes on the seriousness of non-compliance with legislation, which controls what is happening at all stages and which takes responsibility when errors occur. The majority of the Committee, all except the member of the Central Party, considers that the Minister of Defence, by letter to the Committee, the replies to the hearings and the measures taken, has emphasised the seriousness of the matter. She has demonstrated the power of action and efforts to ensure that disposals in the future will have a regulatory framework that is both clear and provides less room for discretion. The majority is satisfied with this. The members of the Committee of the Christian People's Party, the Left, the Socialist Left and the Greens are of the opinion that the responsibility for the specific disposals on which this matter is concerned has still not been recognised by the one who has been properly given responsibility: the head of defence. These members consider that this should be followed up by the Council of State. The members of the Committee of the Workers' Party consider that the State Council must follow up on the fact that the responsibility for the specific disposals in this case is not recognised by the Defence's senior management. With regard to the follow-up responsibilities of the State Council, the members of the Committee from the Right, the Progress Party and the Centre point out to their previous comment that, in view of the established functional distribution between legislative and executive State powers, they will assume that the Council of State will consider the administrative measures to be taken to reduce the risk of recurrence of regulatory breaches. These members do not want the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee to intervene in the defence order lines. The Committee considers that transparency of sales to private operators is essential in order to ensure greater control and verification of compliance with the guidelines, including in particular: For this reason, the Committee shall submit the following proposals: ‘The government is asking for full transparency around the sales processes of the Defence and the sale of out-of-court defence equipment to private operators by publishing what has been sold, purchased, sold, sold and intended for end-use after the sale has been completed.’ As this case shows, it is clear that the sale of defence equipment to private foreign actors poses major challenges. The Committee considers that the time has come for the Storting to address this issue and to promote the following proposals: ‘The government is asked to consider further regulation of the sale of out-of-court Norwegian defence equipment to private operators abroad.’ The Committee stresses that the amendment to the export control legislation which ensures the principle of ‘once military, always military’ is a significant improvement. However, there is a basis and need for stronger improvements for the future and, in this context, the Committee supports the following proposals: ‘The government is asked to evaluate the methods of control and documentation for the sale and export of out-of-court defence equipment to the rest of the world and to provide sufficient information on the foreign buyers.’ The majority of the Committee, all except the member of the Central Party, has been given the impression through correspondence and consultations that it has been difficult to obtain information on what has happened in this matter, and it is alarming that even the Council of State has had difficulties in obtaining correct information when it has been desirable. In this context, the Committee shall promote the following proposals: ‘The Government is asked to take the necessary steps to ensure seamless flow of information between the Minister of Defence, the Minister of Foreign Affairs and the Head of Defence, so that correct and up-to-date information is available when such information is requested.’; The majority of the Committee refers to the opinion of the Defence Chief and representatives of the Defence Department indicating that the defence representatives have a different view of the interface of responsibility regarding their function in the event of the disposal of used defence equipment than the view of the Minister of Defence and the Minister of Foreign Affairs. On this basis, the majority of the Committee shall promote the following proposals: ‘The Government asks the Government to ensure that the defence has the same understanding of the interface of responsibility as the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in relation to the legislation governing the disposal of former military equipment.’ In this case, there are two proposals for proposals such as minority proposals, one from the Left and the Left Party and one from the Left, the Left Party and the Green Party. In its recommendation, the Committee proposes five request decisions, as previously explained. In the Committee's additional position, there is one minority proposal, from the Centre Party. In its recommendation, the Committee proposes a decision. In doing so, I recommend the Committee's recommendations as set out in the Committee's opinions.

Without Dagbladet’s journalist Kristoffer Egeberg, who had an article on 14 June 2014 entitled ‘Frequent ex-pirate uses Norwegian warships’, the control committee or others would hardly have noticed this case. The incidence is roughly obtained from a Crimea novel: ‘The daily newspaper can today reveal how the Norwegian authorities sold several marine vessels to a British safety company registered at a campsite in Surrey. Now the ships are part of a private-powered marine power owned by a former Nigerian rebel leader to hunt pirates and collect taxes for one of the world's most lawless and corrupt countries.» The first article describes how the Ministry of Foreign Affairs turned 180 degrees in connection with the sale of KNM ‘Horten’. First, it was stopped by the then Foreign Minister Gahr Støre, with the reason that it was not only out of date, but also a violation of export legislation to sell Norwegian warships to private actors who could come to arm them and offer security services off the coast of Africa. However, only one year later, a contract was signed with the British company CAS Global on the sale of the six demilitarised missile torpedo boats ‘Geir’, ‘Hauk’, ‘Jo’, ‘Stegg’, ‘Tere’ and ‘County’ of the Defence. The same company received the purchase of the large supporting vessel KNM ‘Horten’, this time with the approval of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. The defense did not check the background of the buyer, which is the company, which is currently in a campground in Surrey, and which later sent the boats to Nigeria, while the Ministry of Foreign Affairs based its assessments on information they received from the Defence Department. This is really the background for a joint committee to open up. Such debts relating to the disposal of military material had to go after. Rightly, the Stortinget asked for military material to be sold at the highest possible price, but not outside the code. Of course, it has never been the Storting's intention that military material we no longer use should equip pirates and military forces we far from want anything to do. After many rounds of letters to the departments, two large control hearings, external investigations and the transmission of material to Ecokrim for further research, we still do not know fully how this could actually happen. The first article in a long series contains many of the key questions the Committee has been seeking answers to: whether such halls are legal or in accordance with the rules of disposal if those in charge of the hall knew what they were to be used for, and whether the Defence Department, the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs are responsible for how the responsibilities within the Defence are the dialogue between the Defence Department and the Ministry of Defence as to whether there is openness and willingness to provide information; It has justly been relatively clear to us how outsourced marine vessels could still be used as military-equipped vessels outside of a country of unrest – a country that has been haunted by terrorism and partly major regional conflicts. But it has also been clear to us that there has been no special willingness in the defence to take the matter fully into consideration, and those involved do not take full responsibility for what has happened. The Minister of Defence has taken action to avoid similar cases, and that is good, but at the same time we note that it is not being treated with the same seriousness from the head of defence. It is alarming that a defense chief does not seem to have taken over the Council of State and the Stortinget's desire for a defence that takes the utmost seriousness that legislation is not being followed, and a defense that controls what is happening at all stages and takes responsibility when errors occur. The Labour Party would stress most strongly the importance of having a defence subject to democratic control. The case spokesperson has gone through the proceedings in the committee, but I still want to make some comments about the process because he has uncovered a thinking in the defence that I think we need to discuss here in Stortinget today. In October 2014, the Committee decided to send the first letter in a row of others to the Minister of Defence and the Minister of Foreign Affairs with questions about all the disclosures in the media. The Minister of Defence chose to stress that all the sales had been carried out before she joined, which turned out to be wrong. In addition, she first informed that all the vessels appeared to have been delivered in accordance with the applicable rules and instructions. However, the Minister of Defence also stated that the export control rules were not complied with. In other words, the vessel was sent out of the country without any use or end use being done, and this was critical, said the Minister of Defence. The Council of State made it clear that an internal working group would be set up to review procedures for halls, and the PwC became engaged in an external investigation of completed halls of military vessels. In later correspondence, the Minister of Defense has written to us that the handling of matters has been flawed, and that the defence's handling has been critical. The Minister of Defence has ensured that measures such as suspension of competence, formalisation of internal proceedings and external communication should be implemented and, as an immediate measure, transferred the powers of disposal of material from the FLO to the Chief of Defence. In other words, she has gradually taken the matter seriously and had a desire to clean up. That's what the committee is pleased with. The first hearing was carried out in April 2015, and as I said, the process itself has been important. Throughout this case, it has simply been given different explanations as to who has been responsible for the disposal of military material. In one phase it was more than suggested that this was about one unit added and one department in the Defence. Then came the unclear signal about who has actually had knowledge of what has gone on. For the Workers' Party, however, some of the explanations have been very elucidating in the sense that they have shown how the views of the cases are broken both internally in the Defence and in themselves outside the Defence. The former defense chief Sunde's opinion of as well as the case is interesting. In the first earring, he said, ‘You have to be careful when exporting military material. Therefore, the disposal of these vessels became an agenda item at the monthly coordination meetings held in the Defence Staff.» He also said: ‘there was no secret that when CAS Global came into the picture, these vessels were intended to be used outside West Africa.’ [[...]] Former Chief of Defense Sunde also confirmed that it is the Chief of Defense who is responsible for the supervision of the FLO as a Department of Defence. The Committee on Control, as we know, is primarily responsible for monitoring the Council of State in such cases, but it is nevertheless necessary to find the background for failure to follow up decisions in the Stortinge, and also to give a picture of how a Council of State could have prevented any breach of the rules. The representative Gruvan was among those in the listening ring who put words on it and asked the following questions to Sunde: ‘You briefed instead of having monthly meetings where this was a point on the agenda. How were the Councils of State informed about the organisation and implementation of the sale of these vessels?’ Harald Sunde said: ‘This question must be addressed to the Ministry of Defence. But my comment is that during these monthly coordination meetings, the Ministry of Defence was an actor, and this was a routine part in which a progress process was discussed monthly." So there's nothing in the first one that means that the department, and therefore the state councils, should have some insight into what had happened. No one questioned whether there had been serious violations of the current rules and instructions. Once again, I will refer you to the former defense chief Sunde: ‘This shows exactly what I was talking about, that there is a serious breach here. This is also information that emerges afterwards. Experience and learning have already been included in the instructions of amended sections 4 and 5. But the best and the safest thing is not to dispose of military material." The leader of the committee, Martin Kolberg, summed it up this way: ‘I don’t think anyone is protesting that this is a violation of the law, and that has also been said very clearly by Sunde, and you have also used the term ‘apologies’ — very gross violations and ‘complaints’. So there is already a great deal of agreement between the former Heads of Defense and the Monitoring Committee on the background to this matter." ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e1275	The issue of the sale of used and out-of-court defence equipment has raised a number of key questions as to whether handling is within the current legislation. There is a question of routines on how to capture errors and defects in the disposal process, on current legislation is good enough and on the extent to which the exchange of information between the Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs has been in line with legislation. But perhaps the most eye-catching in the matter is that none of these questions had been raised if not the press, and in this case the newspaper, had revealed clear breaches of the rules and failed procedures in the defense and between the Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. And as Minister of Defence Eriksen Soreide himself said during the hearing in the Committee on 30 April 2015: ‘The main challenge for me in the case has been that it has been difficult throughout the process to obtain complete information and documentation and to confirm or disclaim evidence and information.’ I will give tribute to the case rapporteur for a thorough job in the matter, and I will therefore not repeat his good review of the many aspects of the matter, but focus on some of the things that the Christian People's Party believes are important questions of principle that this matter raises. The normal instructions for the disposal of used and out-of-court defence equipment state that this should be done in the way that gives the most economic benefit. It is also clear from the guidelines that the Head of Defence is the responsible Head of Unit to obtain export authorisation and end-user declaration. The nature of the material subject to this legislation has also been defined and it is underlined that the Ministry of Defence will be kept informed on an ongoing basis of the relevant matters. No transfer of defence equipment according to the definition may take place before a retail declaration and/or export licence is available. It has also been made clear that the end-user declaration shall contain information on where to use the material and a confirmation that the material will not be resold without the agreement of the Norwegian authorities. There is no doubt that this matter has created an impression of an unculture in the Defence. In the case of disposal cases where one has found deviations from the legislation, it has not only created an impression of unculture, but it has clearly revealed an unculture in which one has not been able to see the seriousness of the situation. It has been so important to sell the material that sales have been prioritised more than the integrity of the disposal and the control of who is the recipient. From a different point of view, it can be argued that it has just been politically desirable, and a broad political desire, that one should dispose of the most material at the best price possible, and that is so far right, but it cannot in any case be made an excuse for circumvention or breach of legislation, as documented in this case. I understand the pressure and the ambiguity that employees face when it comes to solving tasks related to the sale of used and unarranged defence equipment, but the conditions that are now revealed are serious, and that is why the Committee has been thoroughly involved in this. A significant reorganisation of the Defence Logistics Organisation, the FLO, was carried out early in the 2000s. The organisation was significantly reduced in size, savings were made, and former Chief of Defense Harald Sunde said during the hearing on 30 April that he was not in doubt that the deviations of the management during the conversion period were due to the conversion. That makes sense, and we understand that. The conversion can explain a part, it can complete an image, and it can help to inform the case, but of course there is no excuse for a breach of the rule that one is, or has been, in a conversion. There has been no adherence to routines. [[...]] It is not possible, in principle, to take place over such a long period of time if the control systems are adequate.» We have witnessed a systematic failure in which unwanted operators with intent to export to countries that violate the export control code were not caught. In individual cases, insufficient evidence has been found to demonstrate a totally unacceptable lack of risk assessment related to the dossier submitted, a procedure which has given unprofessional operators virtually free to circumvent the legislation without risk. Without examining the situation, it appears that there has been an uncritical belief that the exporter provides the Ministry of Foreign Affairs with correct information. In addition, we are also aware that PwC was involved in an analysis of FLO’s disposal procedures. The Chief of Defense's organisation did a bad job. This is very serious. This is not reliable for the Center Party. The work of the overall authority is most important and must be followed with the greatest thoroughness. That is where the greatest detail needs to be reviewed, because where the decisions are most important, and this has not happened. I recognize it from other things. It's an audit culture that's an unculture. This invites to kick down into the organisation, and the consequence is that the audit does not require top political management to take responsibility. Political management is safeguarded, whether it is documented in this control case that the department's departments were regularly briefed, and I know from experience that the department's departments are briefing political management on the first occasion if important information emerges that needs to be responded to. I am asking for the correction of the Council of State if such an understanding is not correct.
