ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e1575	I welcome the vote and would like to point out that the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, in cooperation with the Ministry of Defence and other stakeholders concerned, has worked on an ongoing basis to clarify all the issues raised by the Committee. This has been important to ensure good interaction and procedures around the future disposal of unarranged defence equipment. It implies, for the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, ensuring that exports are subject at all times to the control imposed by the Export Control Code and in accordance with the practice of the majority of the Grand Duchy. The Committee’s setting covers a number of issues from 2006 to the present. The cases have been thoroughly clarified without any error in the Ministry of Foreign Affairs' case being detected in my opinion. However, there is always a link between learning points and such a comprehensive review of such a wide range of issues as the Committee has contributed to. Common to all cases is that the Ministry of Foreign Affairs has based itself on the information submitted to them by the various exporters in the sales of former defence equipment. Here I would like to point out that the vast majority of these cases relate to the time when civil equipment that would only be subject to licensing through the prisoner-alt paragraph, the current §7(c) of the export control regulation. It is important to stress that, at the time of the application for the matters in question, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs did not have reason to doubt the information provided by the exporter. In this context, it is important to recall that the exporter has a legal obligation to provide correct and complete information, reinforced by the strict criminal provisions of the Export Control Act of up to 5 years in prison and/or fines, which may apply if an exporter provides incorrect or incomplete information in a licence case. Nor shall there be any doubt that, on a general basis, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs takes a serious view of any possible breach of the export control legislation. There are indications that the Ministry of Foreign Affairs has been provided with misleading and possibly incorrect information from operators in the context of the licensing assessments of some of the cases examined. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs has therefore reported the sales of the six MTBs and the KNM ‘Horten’, KV ‘Agder’, MS ‘Åhav’, ‘Loch Ness’ and KV ‘Titran’ to the Riksadvokat as possible offences and actively contributed during ongoing investigations to the best information possible. It is now important to await further consideration by the prosecution authority before drawing the final conclusions on causality in these specific cases. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs attaches great importance to ensuring good information to exporters. This is done through major seminars, in meetings with individual actors and through information material. The Ministry of Defence and Defence have also been the subject of ongoing and preventive information activities on export control legislation. However, I would like to stress that the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in 2014 considered that parts of the export control legislation may have previously provided too much discretion for exporters as to whether or not a product was subject to a licence. In order to ensure that the provisions on licensing obligations for military equipment are as clear as possible, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs chose in September 2014 to strengthen control by introducing a specific licensing obligation for all demilitarised materials which have been specifically modified or developed for military use, irrespective of the state of export from Norway. Once military – always licensing. With this in mind, there is no doubt that demilitarised defence equipment, such as former military vessels converted for civil purposes, is subject to licensing independence from the state at the time of export. I believe that this clarification on licensing obligations has helped to reduce the risk of similar issues in the future by submitting to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs such cases for ordinary licensing assessment. At the same time, I would like to stress that Norway does not practise a ban on the export of out-of-court defence equipment. The Committee recommends that the Government take the necessary steps to ensure a seamless flow of information between the Minister of Defence, the Minister of Foreign Affairs and the Defence Director, so that accurate and up-to-date information is available when such information is desired. The Export Control Code is based on the submission to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the necessary and correct information when assessing the obligation to issue an export licence. Everything else is subject to infringements of the export control legislation with its strict criminal framework. As a consequence, following the change in regulation in 2014, the Ministry of Defence, in cooperation with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, has worked to strengthen the internal procedures of the Defence Agency relating to the disposal of former defence equipment. In the proceedings of the Defence Disposal, a separate series of meetings has been established between the Ministry of Defence, the Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs on pending and planned disposal cases with a view to optimum coordination, and not least a common understanding of the legislation. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs will further contribute to this work with a separate orientation for key defence sector personnel on export control legislation in order to ensure a common understanding of roles, duties, responsibilities and interfaces between stakeholders in line with the Committee’s recommendations. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs feels that the recommendations of the Control and Constitutional Committee, in conjunction with the measures already implemented, will contribute to a reinforced control of exports of out-of-court defence material. I would like to stress that the Ministry will continue its work on the export control legislation in line with the premise of the bulk of the population.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e1686	First, I would like to thank the rapporteur and the committee for the work on both hearings and voting. Over the course of a year and a half, we have now thoroughly dealt with several issues relating to the disposal of unarranged defence equipment. Among other things, we have been through two hearings. This is an important matter, it is a complex matter, and it is a matter that I stated at both hearings that I was happy to have been revealed, because it has given rise to a very necessary cleaning work. The matter is also complex because it partly goes back a long way in time, and although we do not currently have any indications of deliberate retention of information, there is a lack of archiving, lack of documentation and traceability over a long period of time. And it's also made that when you're trying to reconstruct, it's of course complicated because there's no more documents. As you know, parts of this case complex are also being investigated by Ecocrim. Let me stress, as I also did in the hearing, that no one is accused or convicted in the matter, but it has been taken out of court for corruption. At the same time, other aspects of the matter are very simple. There are rules on material disposal and those rules must be followed. It has not always happened in these cases. That is why I have implemented a number of measures, some of which are comprehensive. In addition to the hearings and minutes of the Committee's proceedings and the extensive correspondence with the Committee, much of the detail lies around it. I am therefore not going to spend time looking into the details of the individual matters. I would like to focus here on some of the measures that have been implemented, in addition to the measures that the Minister for Foreign Affairs mentioned in his introduction. In the hearings on 30 April and 27 November last year, and in the comprehensive correspondence with the Committee, I have briefed the Stortinget on the improvement measures that have been taken. I note from the opinions given by the Committee that it is ‘fully satisfied that the Departments and Defence have taken action and that the measures taken are correct and necessary’, and that the follow-up is also welcomed. There are important feedback to the ministry, and there are important feedback in the follow-up work we will continue to do. Let me then briefly describe some of what has been done to ensure proper disposal processes, and here it is important to stress that disposal in this sense is both sale, destruction and donation or transfer free of charge. On 20 November 2014, the Ministry of Defence clarified that the powers relating to the disposal of material should be conferred on the Chief of Defence until further notice and not be delegated to the FLO. After the establishment of the Armed Forces Unit, it is clarified that the powers relating to the disposal of material shall be assigned to the entity centrally and not to the executive branch further down into the organisation. It is stressed that this scheme should ensure independent and independent control of material disposal cases. In the context of the establishment of the new establishment from 1 January, the Ministry has placed great emphasis on good internal control in order to provide the basis for sound management of the material in the future. The need for more strategic management of the material area and a shorter and more direct control line across this field were the background. The Ministry of Defence, in dialogue with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, has drawn up a list of matters which must be clarified before the sale in Norway and before the export of materials. The list was sent to the Defence Agency on 27 April 2015 and is now implemented as part of the Ministry of Defence's material management policy. These so-called checklists specify, among other things. studies on the buyer to be made, the information to be provided to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. The lists further lead to case handlers in the defence sector's departments to ensure that the information between the parties on export control issues is good. It has also, as the Committee is familiar with, explored the legal scope for excluding buyers from auctions. We have also established regular coordination meetings between the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs on the disposal and export of materials to ensure the flow of information and build a common understanding. The defence and defence equipment are also involved here. The Ministry of Defence, supported by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, will hold a special seminar on export controls for all relevant parties in the defence sector as part of the strengthening of competence and further strengthening the established understanding between the actors. The seminar will be held for the first time on 14 June of this year and will be a further contribution to ensuring that the actions undertaken and the improvement work will have the necessary effect. A series of regulatory and routine changes is presented both in the correspondence and in the hearings. I have also assessed a closer regulation and tightening of the disposal of material, and I will come back to it later in the article. The defence continues to review all disposals until 2002. That work will take time, but will help to create the necessary overview. I note that in its comments the Committee writes that questions should be asked about whether it is appropriate to review all matters. Of course, we will consider this on an ongoing basis, but until further notice I want to check these matters, which go even further back in time. So far, the ongoing review has not revealed any malaise, but I have said before and I stress again today that I cannot guarantee that it will not show up any more old things, and I cannot guarantee that there will be any errors in the future either. Although most of the things we're going through now have a long story that started years ago, there's no doubt that I'm taking clean-up responsibility, and I'm taking it seriously. The Ministry follows closely the governance dialogue with the agencies with a view to implementing the measures and not least the impact they have over time. The necessary follow-up is carried out on an ongoing basis. Where appropriate, additional actions will be commissioned by the Heads of State or Government. As you know, the new department of Defence must, among other things, handle discards. All sales of discarded material from the defence sector shall now be made by this department. This helps to ensure that the sale of such material is carried out by an environment with dedicated competence in commercial matters, material management including disposal, and with a focus in this area only. Following the revision of the guidelines on material management in the defence sector by the Ministry in January of State, the responsibilities of the Heads of State in relation to disposal and export have been clarified and comprehensive overviews have been incorporated on the matters to be assessed and clarified in the event of the disposal of materials. The Heads of State or Government in the defence sector may continue to carry out donations of material in some cases, with a sum of up to NOK 30000. However, such donations are subject to strict restrictions. For example, all donations of sensitive material and material subject to licensing requirements shall also be authorised by the Ministry of Defence on a case-by-case basis. The Ministry of Defence cooperates with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Defence to ensure that the necessary measures are put in place and that responsibilities and roles are clearly understood. The hearings of the Control and Constitutional Committee in 2015 and the debate today do not end our work. Improvement measures in the defence sector will continue to be closely followed up by the Ministry until we are sure that the measures have achieved the desired effect, by following established requirements and duly documented. This is ongoing work, which also means that there may be changes in procedures and requirements when we gain experience with the measures implemented. Our aim is to ensure thorough, well-documented and sound management in the defence sector. This is a work we follow continuously, and I want special attention to be paid to the enforcement of the measures taken. The Committee has been kept informed about the issues we have followed up, including after the hearings. On 21 April, I sent two reports that have been notified to the Committee, an updated final report on the disposal of the F-5 fighter aircraft and a final report on the material disposal processes in the sector. Both reports were initiated by the Ministry of Defence as part of our follow-up and improvement of disposal work in the sector. Both reports and the ministry's ongoing work on them have been made known to the Committee through references in previous correspondence, in the hearings and in particular with regard to the report on F-5 fighter aircraft, explicitly in the Committee's comments in the vote of 15 March. Both reports have also been routinely transmitted to the National Audit Office, in line with common practice. In addition, the report on internal procedures for disposal was discussed at both hearings, both in April and November. It was then found that material from the internal audit was being reviewed in the ministry to assess the improvement measures that needed to be implemented. The last part of the ministry’s examination of the reports was the implementation of improvement measures against the agencies in the defence sector. This was formalised on 15 April this year through the so-called ‘Precisions, amendments and additions’, PET, to letters of implementation to the defence sector for the Long-term Plan 2013–2016. That same day I was presented with a summary of the report and the follow-up work by the department. Therefore, it is important that we also added the letter that Økokrim then sent to us, namely that they related to the case of F-5 fighter aircraft do not have an investigation aimed at this disposal. They have also noted that the updated final report of the Ministry of Defence’s internal audit will not be subject to further follow-up by Ecocrim. The internal audit concludes in the final report that the sale carried out in 2015 was approved by the Ministry of Defence, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the US authorities and rejects the claim from the first report that the aircraft may have been disposed of by others, contrary to the legislation. I would also like to add that the National Audit Office routinely receives all our reports from our internal audit department. In other words, the National Audit Office is familiar with the report on internal procedures for the disposal of material and both the preliminary and final report on the sale of F-5 fighter aircraft. None of the two reports revealed maladministration. The recommendations from both reports have already been implemented or are being implemented. The Committee also commented in its additional opinion that the Committee was in favour. In line with the practice, the Ministry of Defence has also published on its websites — while the Committee was receiving the documents — upgraded versions of classified reports. So to the request decisions I to V in the Committee’s opinion of 15 March: first I and III. The members of the Committee did not specifically mention these Roman Numeral Decisions, but I assume that it is not intended, and I intend to mention them. In the opinion, the Committee asks that the government introduce full transparency around the sales processes of the defence and the sale of out-of-court defence material to private operators by publishing what has been sold, the buyer, the sales total and the intentional end-use after the sale has been completed. The majority also asks the government to assess further the regulation of the sale of end-of-life Norwegian defence equipment to private operators abroad. I would like to ask our offices to facilitate the publication of information on the sale of material to private operators. The government will appropriately return information to the Storting on how best to operate such a scheme and what needs to be taken into account, as appropriate, in response to information related to confidentiality. As regards the sale of discontinued Norwegian defence equipment to private operators abroad, I have, as I announced in the hearing on 27 November, made a more detailed assessment of the regulation of this. All sales of licensed material to non-defence actors shall now, following the revised material management guidelines, be subject to FD approval on a case-by-case basis. In addition, I have assessed the possibilities for further regulation of the future disposal of material. I have decided that any future sale of material by brokers will be approved by the Ministry of Defence in the individual case. I have also decided that all sales in Norway to foreign operators should be treated as sales to the rest of the world. In such cases, it is necessary to clarify any licensing obligation with the U.D. and to obtain approval from the Ministry of Defence. Another constraint is that all sales in Norway of licensed material shall be based on provisions that any subsequent export of the material shall be subject to approval by the Ministry of Defence. As a starting point for the further disposal of materials, the sale of licensed materials to the rest of the world shall preferably only be made to authorised national authorities and operators authorised in the country of destination. All such sales to the rest of the world of licensed material will continue to require approval by the Ministry of Defence. Such a system ensures increased control of the buyer of out-of-court defence equipment and at the same time allows for the disposal of material for particularly suitable purposes, such as: to international relief organisations. I have further to assess a stop in all the auctioning of larger material, such as. trucks, belt wagons and larger vessels, which means that direct sales must then be made instead of auctioning. In my view, this will contribute to the necessary control of the use and end-use of such materials. Such regulation of the sale of licensed materials to the rest of the world and stop in the auctioning of larger materials will further tighten the possibility of selling materials to private operators abroad. It will continue to be possible to sell licensed and larger materials after such regulation, but then through direct sales under the control of and after closer assessment in the ministry. I still want to keep the possibility of donation open, but donation should follow the same tightening that I have now explained. Here it is also important to add that over time experience can show that you want to do things differently, but I think this is a measure that is now right to be implemented. The representative, Lundtigen, had a very central point in his statement, namely that the sale of belt wagons came particularly as a result of criticism of a lot of destruction of belt wagons in the early 2000s. This is the dilemma of these assessments. Therefore, it is important to note that a reduction in the sale of material as a result of such changes will lead to lower revenues from sales, leading to higher costs of storage and destruction. It is difficult to calculate the exact economic impact as it is, among other things, depends on the type of material, it depends on the market situation, but the consequence will be that any resources that would otherwise have come from sales and been channelled for higher priority purposes, inter alia. operational activity and activity, in which case it will not be possible to do so. The framework for the tightening up of the sales of materials will be clarified by means of the budget proposal for the autumn. To give some figures from the last few years: in 2013, materials were sold for 129.4 million. SEK, 2014 for SEK 144,9 million. kr. The reporting for 2015 indicates that it has been sold for gross 44.7 million. kr. The budget for 2016 is 23 million. kr, but the first figures indicate that 6,3 million have been sold so far. kr. It's just to give the committee an idea of what sums we're talking about. I'm going so far to II, IV and V. In this setting, the Swedish Government is asking the Government to take the necessary steps to ensure seamless information flows between the Minister of Defence, the Minister of Foreign Affairs and the Head of Defence, so that correct and up-to-date information is available when such information is requested. The Government is also asked to ensure that the defence has the same understanding of the interface of responsibility as the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in relation to the legislation governing the disposal of former military equipment. The majority further requests the government to evaluate the methods of control and documentation for the sale and export of out-of-court defence equipment to the rest of the world and to have sufficient information on the foreign buyers. As I've been in the past, on 1 January of this year we established the defence equipment department, and it is now this department that is responsible for all sales of defence equipment. We have established procedures and meeting series between the Ministry of Defence and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in which representatives of the Defence and Defence Matters participate. This is to ensure just a seamless flow of information and not least early clarifications on the sale and export of materials. As I've been in the hearings, some of the challenge has been that we've only been based on deviation reporting and not really dealing with matters on an ongoing basis, and that's why things don't come up unless there's someone who discovers that it's a red flag. This is an attempt to rather be proactive and talk about the issues before any exports. We have also introduced checklists for the disposal of materials in cooperation with the U.D. Procedural agents in the defence sector shall use the lists in order to ensure correct and necessary information between the parties. We are also conducting, as I mentioned, a special seminar to ensure that the measures taken and the improvement work will have the necessary impact. The Committee's decisions will therefore, in my opinion, be well taken care of through the measures I have now outlined. The note in the supplementary position of a unanimous committee to follow up the F-5 sale is also made through these measures – and the fact that of the eight measures in the F-5 report mentioned, four are already implemented and the four others are being implemented. There shall be no doubt in any of the actors as to the requirements applicable to the disposal and export of defence equipment. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs manages the export control legislation and the defence sector relates to the provisions in force. As both the head of defence and I stated in both hearings, the defense was responsible for providing the U.D. sufficient information to assess the licensing obligation in these cases. I trust that the Chief of Defence and other Heads of State in the defence sector will comply and contribute to the proper implementation of the measures now in place. This implies implementing the necessary actions in their own organisation, so that adequate resources and competences follow the tasks assigned to them. An important reason for this is also clearly stated by the head of defence during the hearings. In the first hearing on 30 April, the head of defence was very clear that there were shortcomings both in the follow-up to legislation and in the proper handling of cases. He points to breaches of both the normal and export control rules. He goes on to tell about what he feels is a system failure that should have been asked control questions at earlier times, but no one in the Defense asked control questions about. The Chief of Defence is aware that the Defence Officer is responsible for carrying out the necessary investigations in the material to be transferred to the U.D. In the first hearing, the Chief of Defense further states that he stresses that they take seriously the facts that are critical here. The next hearing also made it clear from the head of defence – and I think that is quite right and important – that it is their responsibility, that is, the responsibility of the defence, to provide the right information in this case. So the head of defense also says that there has not been enough control, and that there has not been enough emphasis on this control or these requirements in the normal instructions. The Chief of Defense also concludes by saying that the Defence Logistics Organisation did a job that in many areas was not good enough, and the Chief of Defense says clearly: ‘I’m sorry, it’s my responsibility.’ I believe there are important feedback from the head of defence, and there are feedback I put into the further work we are doing, together with the Defence and the head of the department who is responsible. I will continue to work to ensure the safe disposal of material and, in particular, good, traceable work processes. The Ministry of Defence will evaluate the measures either undertaken or planned to be taken in the area of disposal after they have had their effect for some time, and not least when the effect can be measured subsequently. The clearing work will not end with this debate. It goes on with the same strength. It is incredibly important that this work continues to be followed up. I take the cleaning work very seriously, and we will continue to do so, and new measures will be implemented if necessary. Then I will end by pointing to something that more people in the committee have been involved in, and as I think there is reason to stress and also to say that I fully agree, namely that one of the greatest challenges has been to obtain information and not least to be able to confirm or invalidate the information that is coming. Much of the reason for this was that I was thinking about the whole opening of my post, namely that there is a lot of documentation that is not available and that is not easy to reconstruct. One of the things we do, among other things. has been done by the Ministry, asking for transparency in the currency register – we have received a two-fold refusal – because it could provide more information about some of the transactions. At the same time, neither the department nor the control committee are set up as investigative bodies and therefore do not have all the warrants and powers, but I feel confident that this is a matter such as this. Ecocrime can do something in its investigation of these issues, because there is information that will be valuable and that could help to shed more light on this. I said in the hearings that there is one thing I wish I could do differently, it is to have given more information earlier, if I had it. It has taken time, and it took particular time at the beginning, actually to get an overview of the information and get verified or verified if that information was correct. I feel that it is now in a completely different state, in the sense that it is well-lit, except for the areas I have been talking about, where there is still no documentation. We have, in my opinion, come as far as we can come to reveal this information, but this case complex and parts of it are, as I said in the Ecocrim investigation, and I would assume that it is likely that Ecocrim also has methods and wants to find information that we do not have access to.

Nevertheless, we see that especially in cities, it is a challenge to make it work. midwives have a very heavy workload. This is key staff who are important for the beginning of a child's life. I also have to deal with one thing that worries me a lot, and that may seem like a little problem, but that is an incredible tragedy for all those who experience it. It's about the stillbirths we have, the kids who die in the stomach of the last part of their pregnancy. Here, too, we are not able to come here in Norway. It's not that evolution always goes to the worse, but it's had very little progress. We see that countries like Finland, Denmark and the Netherlands have systematically worked to reduce the numbers of young people who lose their lives in the last part of their pregnancy. This is a tragedy for every family, and it's kids that we can't lose. The Netherlands has managed to achieve a 7 pst reduction. per year since 2000. If Norway had managed to take care of babies as well as they do in the Netherlands, 60 children would have been rescued. What is done then is systematic analysis and research on those very groups. In 2013, there were 2.3 stillbirths in the last 28 weeks of pregnancy per 1,000. That means there were 140 kids who died. In 2014, it was increased to 2.8 per 1,000, which is 170 children that we lost. It has to be researched. We have to try to stretch to be as good at this as you are in many other countries. Then there is something that strikes me, when I visit both large and small hospitals and see the challenges there. For example, I was. in Flekkefjord for the week that passed, which has a maternity ward that has both gynecologists and surgeons present, but which conducts very active screening and follow-up of each individual to know whether there is a birth suitable for their hospitals, or whether a person should be sent to a larger hospital with more professional staff. What has struck me is that if you plan to have a birth in a hospital that has the special competence, you don't get that kind of screening. What happens to them in the districts to be born is that they get a completely different type of follow-up and assessment of whether it is a difficult birth than those who are going to be born in a hospital that is tipping top and ‘fit for fight’ for all the challenges you face. That's why there are many stories in the big-city hospitals about parents who don't feel well enough followed up, and many stories about mothers who are inside because they're worried, are told to take it easy, and then they come back a few days later, and then life is gone. We have to deal with this. This we have to have a plan to stop. If we're going to be as good as the Netherlands, Finland and Denmark to get that number down, maybe there's something we have to bet on earlier in pregnancy. My journey around Norway has made me feel sometimes that many mothers in the districts get a better follow-up than many mothers in the big cities, and we cannot accept that. We can't accept that because a person lives in a city, one has to bear that one doesn't get there, while we use a lot of resources to ensure that everyone reaches birth planned in hospitals elsewhere in the country. My challenge and my question to the Minister of Health is: how are we going to be able to work with these fields to get this similarity, and is it that mothers in cities get a worse follow-up than mothers in many smaller hospitals?
ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e2073	First, I would like to thank the interpellant for raising this important discussion. A birth is one of the greatest events in life and is usually attended by great expectations. The expectation of having a healthy child is very often present, but most also have a expectation of the very birth experience. A good birth experience provides a basis for better mental health in the postpartum period and better interactions between mother and child. Studies have shown that the woman’s own perception of birth is more important to her mental state in the future than any medical complications that arise in the maternity ward. It is therefore very important to facilitate the optimal birth experience of the unborn. I am therefore pleased that the representative Skei Grande is asking questions about some of the conditions that could create challenges in achieving this goal. The results of Norwegian maternity care are very good. Child and mother mortality is among the lowest in the world. We have lower incidences of cesarean cuts than many other western countries. We can be proud of such results. But there should be more work to ensure that the woman and her partner get a good birth experience. The main requirements for maternity care are reflected in the Annual Report A happy event, from 2009, and the Directorate-General for Health A safe food supply, from 2010. Health businesses have done a good job of improving the quality of birth care in the years following these requirements. These include: the establishment of accompanying services for pregnant women living far away from the place of birth, and selection criteria have been introduced, which were also addressed by the interpellant, for which women can be born at different types of food institutions. These measures have contributed significantly to improving the quality of birth care. Among other things, the number of transport births has dropped from 193 in 2010 to 124 in 2014. On a national basis, that means 0.2 pst. of all births are transport births. The proportion is naturally higher in counties with a long distance from the birth institution, but the number is highest in the major cities where the interpellant was located. Parental care poses a particular challenge by the fact that births are usually not fully planned. This leads to wide variations in the number of births that address the maternity wards. The variations are large from day to day, but there are also seasonal variations with the highest number of births in the summer, for which the Stortinget has a responsibility, for which the interpellant was involved. One example is the maternity ward at Akershu University Hospital. It is born from 5 to 29 children a day. These variations make it extra demanding to plan the crew. The number of births that can be accepted at the large women’s clinics is also influenced by the availability of space at the neonatal wards. Births are covered by the system of free treatment choices. Thus they can choose the place of birth. But it happens that sometimes the selected food institution does not have the capacity to accept the woman on the day of birth. In this case, arrangements must be in place to ensure that the pregnant woman is referred to another maternity ward with free capacity. This issue is most topical in the Oslo area, where there are several food departments within a limited geographical area. Cooperation between food establishments in and around Oslo has therefore been established to relieve each other where necessary. It is the food institution to which the woman addresses, who is responsible for providing space in another hospital and for obtaining ambulances if necessary. In other parts of the country, the problem is less topical. The health companies have informed me that birthmarks are not rejected at the women's clinics in Trondheim, Bergen or Stavanger. Occasionally, the birthing person does not reach the hospital until the birth is due. In 2014, there were 194 births, or 0.3 pst. of all births, unplanned home births. In Hordaland and Rogaland, the proportion of unplanned home births was slightly higher than the national average. In Oslo and South-Trøndelag it was a little lower than the national average, but the differences between the counties were small. Food institutions state that the main reason for unplanned home births is simply that birth is faster than the woman expects, not that she is rejected in the maternity ward. There is no over-representation of foreign-language birthers among those who are born unplanned at home. Several food establishments indicate that they have a lower threshold to accept births if there are language or communication problems. The Women's Clinic in Bergen has opened an offer called Speech. The offer is for women who are unsure whether they are born or feel unsafe at home. Here the woman is offered to stay after the assessment and examination of the midwife. During the hours the woman is on Speech, it will be clarified whether she is in birth, and she will either be transferred to the maternity ward or be able to go home. This is, in my opinion, a good measure that others should also consider. The women's clinic in Bergen has also changed the telephone contact procedures. Instead, the midwife who runs the guard phone takes the woman to the assessment to make sure she is not turned down. The same applies if there are language problems — she is also considered. These measures have worked. Haukeland University Hospital has recorded a decline in the number of women who have responded that they have felt rejected when they called the Women’s Clinic. All major food establishments have access to interpreting services. But since births are most often an acute event, it is not always possible to obtain interpreters during an ongoing birth. When it is possible to plan, interpreters are ordered or telephone interpreters are used. The food department at Bærum Hospital has launched a project that looks at the possibility of developing communication tools to secure the woman during birth. It shall also ensure that important information is provided in an understandable way — from patient to healthcare professional and back again. If this project produces good results, it should also be possible to use others. National quality requirements for maternity care provide a clear framework for the development of food supply. However, it is the health companies that are responsible for the compliance of the offer, so that the birthers can have a safe and good birth experience. High birth rates and fluctuations in the number of births place great demands on the organisation and staff planning of the large food establishments. This is work in progress today and should be intensified. There is also a need to provide more family-based maternity care so that the woman’s partner can be present during the maternity period. The use of interpreters should probably also be increased beyond what is happening today. Unplanned births outside the hospital are fortunately rare, and you see the big cities as a whole, they are not different from the country as a whole in terms of the birth rate, but we see, as the interpellant was about, that it is where the largest number is. The quality of food supply is a topic that is often on the agenda in the discussion between myself and the health regions. It will be in the future as well. The aim is that the offer is to be equivalent, to ensure a good result for mother and child while at the same time facilitating a good birth experience for the woman and her partner. It is good that we now receive attention around the offer of birthers in the big cities. As the interpellant was involved, there has been a lot of attention around the food supply in the districts. We also see that the birth message and the requirements that have led to a very clear awareness of the smaller food departments, good screening of the birthing and not least a continuity in the follow-up. For example, in Nordfjord in Sogn and Fjordane, we see that the municipality and health companies have joint employees of midwives, so that it is the same midwives who are responsible for them in the municipality, who also have the accompanying service, and who meet them afterwards. It gives security, and it gives continuity, and I'm going to think that the major hospitals here have a lot to learn from the smaller hospitals.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e2320	First, I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation in dealing with this matter. The representatives of Bollestad and Toppe promote in this matter a proposal for the Grand Government to set up a wide-ranging sample to prepare a proposal for a national alcohol policy strategy, in order to ensure that Norway reaches the agreed target of at least 10 pst. reduction in harmful alcohol consumption by 2025. They want to make the choices and choices of alcohol policy more visible in order to achieve the policy objectives of reducing consumption and limiting harm. A Joint Committee recognises that alcohol is a risk factor for lifestyle diseases and can lead to premature death, as well as that alcohol consumption also has other adverse effects such as insecurity, harm and social stress. The Committee agrees that it is in the interests of society to limit consumption and that there is a link between the total consumption of alcoholic beverages and the extent of harm caused by their use. Alcohol is indisputably the most commonly used drug. Therefore, it is important that we have an ongoing debate on the adverse effects. Alcohol use has a negative impact on public health. Excessive alcohol consumption affects the health status of both individuals and the general population. The entire Committee agrees that Norway has joined the World Health Organisation target of a reduction of at least 10 pst. in harmful use by 2025. However, a large majority of the Committee does not support the proposal for a representative to draw up a national alcohol strategy. That doesn't mean that the majority is against a comprehensive alcohol policy. This is more likely to indicate that a number of measures have already been taken that you will see working together. The most important thing we can do is to pursue a predictable alcohol policy where the main lines are fixed. This will also achieve the widest possible support in the population. If the majority of the population loses faith in a strict alcohol policy, then the upsurge of it will fall. This means that strange schemes such as the Vinmonopoly, a high tax regime, advertising bans and opening time restrictions could be challenged. These instruments have had a good effect. Norway has low consumption in the European context, and the trend is that consumption among young people is falling. Therefore, it will also be important in the future to have these means to provide for a limited consumption of alcohol. Although minor adjustments are made in some instruments, the core is solid and contributes to limiting alcohol use. The size has dealt with several government communications aimed at improving public health and reducing the damage and disadvantages caused by alcohol use. Both the public health communication and the drug escalation plan contain new measures to be taken in the future to achieve the alcohol policy objectives. As examples of measures to contribute to the reduction of alcohol use, both an overall committee and the Council of State in their letter to the Committee are moving forward to strengthen the working life of the drug-prevention arena to enter the central arenas in order to prevent adverse drug use, for example: on pregnancy control and after accidents and violence, development work to establish a programme for public health work in municipalities with a focus on mental health and drug prevention and to establish indicators to follow the development of the objective of reducing premature death due to non-infectious diseases. The aim and expectations are that the existing instruments and the new measures together contribute to the rational and limited use of alcohol. At the same time, I want to add that enough alcohol has come to stay. Everyone here in this room wants the best possible public health and realizes that it is important to limit the use of alcohol in the population. This is closely linked to the rest of the public health policy. We also agree to work to achieve the goal of at least 10 pst. reducing the harmful consumption of alcohol by 2025. The majority of the Committee, however, dispute the need for an additional sample, this time in order to develop a strategy to achieve this objective. In general, we in the Health Committee should carefully assess the situation before ordering new strategies, action plans, investigations or samples. As Arild Aspøy points out in the Evening Post on 21 May this year: in the first two sessions of the current period, there have been almost five times as many request decisions compared to the previous government. Only in the last year’s session were 192 such decisions.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e2350	The Labour Party is pleased that the Christian People's Party and the Center Party are once again lifting up the alcohol policy in the landfill. It's an important theme that may not have been lifted well enough in the past. The working party also said clearly when we dealt with the Ruplan, that the relationship between alcohol and health should have a much larger place. Alcohol is the drug that poses the greatest health challenges. It is legally and often linked to social relationships that are perceived as positive for many, but also as a challenge for a large minority who face challenges with their alcohol consumption. Alcohol not only represents a health risk but creates a demanding growth for over 70,000 children who grow up with parents who are struggling with alcohol problems. A high proportion of acute health damage, accidents and youth violence are caused by direct or indirect alcohol. The Labour Party agrees with the Christian People's Party and the Centre on a lot in terms of the need for a more offensive alcohol policy, but we disagree on whether a national alcohol policy strategy is the most appropriate tool. If we are to succeed in reducing the harm caused by uncontrolled alcohol consumption, we believe it is important to see the different drug challenges in context, and that alcohol becomes an essential part of the future overall drug plan of the Storting. The working party places particular emphasis on research showing that alcohol is the main source of illicit drugs and that most drug addicts have a polydrug use. We therefore believe that an overall approach to the prevention, treatment and after-care of various forms of drug dependence is important. This also applies to the misuse of legal medicinal products, which, in our opinion, receive far too little attention. We have also learned that action plans often have their limitations by not being able to see the whole, and are not becoming a good tool for politicians who both locally and nationally want to take action, both with prevention and with the development of comprehensive treatment provision and aftercare. The working party believes it is more appropriate to strengthen the place of alcohol in the Ruplan rather than to split the field up. The working party has proposed to strengthen the role of decision-makers in preventing health damage caused by alcohol. Alcohol is still taboo, and especially for many women, it is related to shame. There are still many prejudices among decision-makers about the use of knowledge-based screening methodologies, and the decision-makers lack knowledge of treatment options and correlation between alcohol and the incidence of various somatic and psychiatric health problems. We believe it is necessary to develop up-to-date guides to determine how to get the good talk on such a vulnerable topic. Early intervention is an effective prevention of drug problems and dependence. We also call for more research into the relationship between alcohol and health. Working life is an important venue for drug prevention. We are committed to strengthening the Akan work and the work of the parties. We have also called for a better knowledge base for local politicians in particular, so that they can take the right action and have the opportunity to see what works. During the consultation, Actis showed the World Health Organisation’s measuring indicators that have been used in several countries. We challenge the government to develop measurement indicators based on those used by the World Health Organisation, often supplemented by indicators that we are particularly concerned with in Norwegian health policy. In this way, we can see developments between the different countries and, not least, both local and national politicians can know which actions should be taken and whether they have an impact.

It is tempting to quote the English journalist Gilbert Keith Chesterton, who lived from 1874 to 1936. He said, "Go to the glass with your joy, but not to the bottle of your grief." Because it's a little about the fact that the traditional Norwegian holy stuff has changed. This shall happen to people at risk or in the process of developing a problem, or already having mild or moderate drug problems, and of course to those with severe drug dependence, as well as their relatives. The escalation plan provides an overview of the main challenges also for alcohol consumption and abuse, as well as short-term and long-term strategies to meet them. The escalation plan covers three priority areas: early response, treatment and after-care. Special focus is given to children and young people, relatives and working lives. We must learn to look for danger signals in young people early, take responsibility and take action, including: by intervention. ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e2626	As rapporteur of the case, I am pleased that there is cross-political support for the content of this proposition; that the right to the free choice of the rehabilitation site should include private and non-profit rehabilitation institutions that have agreements with regional health establishments. It is entirely in line with the proposal put forward by the Stoltenberg II Government in Prop. 118 L for 2012–2013 in the spring of 2013. On the basis of this proposition, the Directorate-General for Health was commissioned in April of the same year to explore possible solutions for the free choice of training and rehabilitation in the specialist health service. The Directorate was asked to describe various professional issues and to address legal, organisational and economic consequences. Follow-up missions were given in August 2013, and responses to this mission were received in April of the following year. That's what's now at the root of the grips you're taking in this proposition. It is an explicit objective that the right to free choice of the site of rehabilitation should contribute to the better use of the total capacity of these key services, and also to reduced waiting times — actually reduced waiting times. At the same time, we are aware of the fact that several health businesses have largely practised free rehabilitating options for a longer period of time. Therefore, a legislative change in this field is largely in line with both applied practice and with the views expressed by most hearing bodies. We have noted with interest that the proposition does not imply that private rehabilitation institutions should be approved under Regulation No. 1232 of 29 October 2015 on the freedom of private undertakings to provide specialist health services in return for payment by the State, and thus not being able to participate in the free choice of the place of rehabilitation, but to allow such institutions to be phased in later. Nor shall x-ray and laboratory services be subject to the new rules on free choice of treatment, but according to the applicable law will have a basis in operators who have an agreement with the public. We are pleased that today's government with this proposition is following up the initiative of the red-green government. There are various reasons why the Workers' Party has considered that free rehabilitation options had to be investigated before the experiment was presented, as was the case during the previous government period. Among other things, it is important for us that the free choice of the place of rehabilitation is an indispensable prerequisite for the existence of good institutions throughout the country, if freedom of choice is to be real to all. In the previous debate on whether rehabilitation should be part of the free-hospital choice scheme, several of them argued. Some smaller rehabilitation institutions were concerned that this could remove the basis from the decentralised provision of rehabilitation and lead to different access to rehabilitation services. Such issues are also addressed in the present proposition, so it is important to keep this in focus also in the future. This document shows with all clarity how unclear and confusing the government’s communication around the system of free choice of treatment is. ‘Free choice of hospitals’ is now referred to as ‘free choice of treatment’, which is initially entirely unproblematic. However, when, at the same time as ‘free treatment’, the government grants completely different rights to patients than ‘free hospital choice’, this becomes both erroneous and misleading. Regulation No. 1232 of 29 October 2015 defines the new civilly-initiated ‘free choice of treatment’ scheme. And it is – as previously noted – only designed to grant rights to the private actors who are alleged to be able to treat patients on public account and who do not have an agreement with a health company. The patient's right to choose is, in fact, the same as before. The content of the proposal we are dealing with today may indicate that the government has noted that ‘free treatment choice’ under Regulation 1232 has not worked so far, and that therefore ‘free treatment choice’ — without any regulation — has been chosen for rehabilitation, x-ray and laboratory services. We think it's very good.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e2641	Today, the Stortinget adopts a change in the patient and user rights law that was first proposed by the Right in 2012. Maybe you just have to assume that it takes four years to get a majority of good proposals here in Stortinget – or is it about this being one of several examples that the red-greens in Norwegian politics are like a heavy cling to the foot when it comes to putting the patient's rights first? Today, it is gratifying that the entire Committee recommends to the Stortinge to decide that patients in need of rehabilitation should be able to choose freely between public and private institutions with agreement and that the change also implies that rehabilitation can be phased into the new authorisation system for free treatment choices. Today is a victory for patients and for all patients who today do not have the freedom to choose for themselves where to get their rehabilitation. Until now, patients have been able to choose rehabilitation in various public hospitals, but they have not had the right to choose freely from private rehabilitation institutions. From now on, we're changing it. Now we make sure that all patients get this right, no matter where they live in the country. In the right, we well remember the patient history from 2011 and 2012, when MS patients in Health Nord were deprived of the possibility of rehabilitation in health regions other than their own. This meant that they could no longer recover the costs of rehabilitation at the MS-Center in Hakadal. Being able to be rehabilitated by those institutions that specialize in diseases such as MS means a lot to patients, not only medically, but also by meeting peers in the same situation across the country. It allows to link links that can be invaluable for those who live with severe chronic disorders and who may need a network to rely on in their everyday lives. Now Health Nord reversed this in 2013 and signed a new agreement with the MS-Center, but at the same time they reduced the number of places. This meant that while patients in the north needed rehabilitation throughout the year, places were usually used up before the end of the year. It resulted in several vacancies, while patients needed it. I mention this today because this is the very core of what we are now adopting. If we are to provide an equivalent rehabilitation service for patients throughout Norway, they must be given the right to choose for themselves. In the first instance, this right is extended to public and private institutions throughout the country which have agreements with regional health establishments. Later, we hope in the Right, of course, that the freedom to choose will be further extended to apply regardless of whether the institution already has an agreement with regional health companies. We need to use all the good forces in our society. In the right, we are primarily concerned that patients receive the necessary treatment and rehabilitation, of high quality and as quickly as possible. Whether the institution is public, non-profit or whole-private, it doesn't matter to us as long as the quality is good. In Norway, we're good at saving lives, but we don't give patients good enough help to live. Many people experience an impressive health service in an acute disease phase — after a heart attack, a stroke or a traffic accident. But when the blue lights have been turned off, many people feel that the help is getting worse. Rehabilitation, which offers the opportunity to master everyday life despite injury or illness, is an important part of healthcare, but today many feel that the interaction between specialist health services and municipalities is not seamless enough. On our guard we have already done a lot with this. The grip of primary health notification, with greater teamwork and less silo-organisation of municipal relief services, is an important step on the way. The same is true of the law of physiotherapy and ergotherapy competence in municipalities. But the case today is primarily about the patient's right to greater freedom of choice in the rehabilitation of specialist health services. We look forward to the government bringing in the long-term recovery plan. It will give it the important promise and point forward. Rehabilitation is a health policy area that we have a great responsibility to develop, and in the Right there has been a year-long commitment to lifting just this field. Today, we can see that a new and important milestone has been reached in this work.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e2695	Rehabilitation is an important and central part of many patient pathways. Health regions buy services for more than one and a half billion dollars each year in the private rehabilitation institutions. This will be changed by the amendment of the law today and is therefore an important part of building the patient’s healthcare. A right to choose between public enterprises and private rehabilitation institutions that have an agreement with health regions is now established. This means that all patients are given the opportunity to choose a rehabilitation institution, regardless of where they live in the country. It's a development that we have to go into to see what's the reason for that. But to draw the bastant conclusion that some people make in this discussion, that this is done for tactical reasons, that it is unfortunate for patients, etc., is not the basis for this. The Health Directorate will be instructed to find out what this turn is about. However, the figures for the trend in waiting time are comparable over time. Waits are going down, and the number of waits is going down.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e2884	Noise and mental health problems and suffering are one of the major health and social challenges in Norway. Towards half of the inhabitants of Norway develop mental health problems during their lives, and there is an extent that requires qualified help. Between 15 and 20 pst. In Norway, children and young people have reduced their function due to mental health problems. People with extensive drug and mental health problems live more than 20 years shorter than the average in the population. The government itself has spoken. priority should be given to drugs and mental health in the now proposed municipal proposal. In the specialist services, trust is given to the Golden Rule, which aims to ensure higher annual growth in drug and mental health treatment at regional level than for somatics. The community health service shall benefit from the newly attached escalation plan for drug use, but a corresponding mental health escalation plan is not planned. According to the Roadmap of the drug field, the government assumes that around 122,000 people can be considered as difficult-to-use in the housing market. In addition, about 6,200 are missing a fixed space to bow. People with drug problems and/or mental disorders are overrepresented among the homeless. A 2012 survey showed that more than half of the homeless depend on drugs and up to 40 pst. have mental disorders. Many users are developing a stable and safe housing as the most important first step in a rehabilitation process. A 2013 SINTEF report on unnecessary admissions, ready-to-print patients and the cooperation on single patients with drug problems and mental disorders show that a significant number of entries could be avoided if municipal facilities, especially housing, were in place. 7 pst. of patients in the field of mental health protection and 1 pst. in the case of multi-disciplinary specialised processing, registered as ready-to-print. Most people are waiting for a place to live. A lot of people need a 24-hour bus offer. A report prepared by the Norwegian Institute of Urban and Regional Research from 2015 shows that 60 pst. patients in multidisciplinary specialised treatment have resided, while 40 pst. are registered as homeless at end of treatment. Half of the users of lowthreshold services live, and the other half are homeless. SINTEF also emphasises in its report that there is a significant potential to avoid installations through the establishment of adequate municipal provision, especially housing, and that it is indicated that up to 25 pst. the inclusion in mental health protection could be avoided by a stronger municipal provision. The interaction reform, introduced in 2012, outlined the following key health challenges: Health services are characterised by too little effort to limit and prevent disease. The need for a holistic and coordinated service for patients is not adequately addressed, and demographic development and change in the disease picture mean that we need to change the way health services are provided. The reform is referred to as a ‘directional reform’, where a running one must evaluate the means against the objectives. The reform assumed that the expected growth in the need for a comprehensive healthcare service would have to find a solution as far as possible in the municipalities and that a greater proportion of the resource growth in the health sector would come here in the coming years. Financial arrangements have been put in place, pay-as-you-go patients and municipal co-financing to support the desired development – the latter has now been phased out by today’s government – while at the same time an obligation has been imposed on municipalities to create municipal immediate aid beds. Russ and psychiatry were part of the interaction reform – many say the opposite – but the financial arrangements were initially confined to somatic patients, given that they already had a good municipal health and care service with regard to the care of the elderly. The intention and main challenges of the interaction reform are and were correct and necessary – I will clarify that. An increased commitment to the community health service, better prevention and better interaction with specialist services is also a major challenge today. The national audit has now examined the use of resources and the quality of health services following the implementation of the interaction reform. It shows that municipalities have increased their capacity to a small extent and strengthened their competence following the implementation of the interaction reform. It shows that within the Russian and Psychiatric areas there is no provision in the municipalities as daily space is reduced. 70 pst. of the municipalities have only slightly increased the number of annual work in the drugs and psychiatry field, while every 10 beds in hospitals have been laid down. This I think is disturbing information. The 2017 municipal bill states that the government has suspended the imposition of payment obligations on ready-to-depreciate patients in mental health protection and multidisciplinary specialised treatment, and will introduce it at the earliest opportunity in 2018, and provides the basis for the decision that: ‘Unlikely, there is too much uncertainty in the data base to be able to implement the scheme without a significant risk to the provision of services to users with extensive and complex needs.’» The government, as announced in the primary health report, also states that municipal immediate assistance should be provided daily for mental health and drug use, but as early as 2017. The central party agrees that as long as we have financial incentives to manage the treatment process for the benefit of patients, these should in principle be diagnostically independent. At the same time, we think it is alarming that one now expands these payment schemes that we know is a driver to change the treatment process, but we are not sure that the municipal health service is developed and in advance. For we know today that the annual work effort in municipal mental health work is greatly increased through the escalation plan from 1998–2008. However, according to a 2013 SINTEF report, the annual effort has not increased since 2007, while patients are being released more quickly from the specialist healthcare service. And already many municipalities in the reporting state to the Health Directorate that the specialist services are being built up faster than the municipalities have the framework to build up. This is entirely in line with what the Riksrevision also points to. In such a situation, I ask whether it is a responsible health policy to introduce the economic instruments of interaction reform as early as 2017-2018, in an area that has been criticised for being undersized.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e2925	The Representative Tope is addressing an important theme. In the evaluation of the supply of people with mental health challenges and drug dependence, more than 24 hours a day in mental health protection and interdisciplinary specialised drug treatment alone can be considered. Although the interpellant is right to reduce the number of daily places in mental health protection in the period 2010–2014, the number of stays in the district psychiatric centres and hospitals increased by nearly 3 pst. In 2014 it was treated 10 pst. more patients in mental health protection than in 2010. The number of days spent in multidisciplinary specialised treatment increased by more than 10 pst. during the same period. Our government's ambition is to create the patient's healthcare. For people with mental health challenges or drug dependence, it is about facilitating independence, security and the ability to live a good life with the best possible quality of life. It doesn't do it through long-term housing, but by helping people where they live. Let me give you a clear example: active outreach treatment team. Seeking treatment teams based on the ACT model are an offer for the very sickest mentally ill, i.e. those who often have a drug problem in addition and who do not benefit from traditional treatment in mental health protection. By facilitating follow-up to the patient’s premises and arenas, the number of days spent in specialist health care is halved in two years. For this group of patients, this is a huge decrease. These patients were on average admitted more than 140 days a year. It's more than a third of a year in a hospital. About two thirds of these days they were placed under compulsion. No one shall tell me that this gives continuity, predictability and security or facilitates an independent and normal life. Now the system of ACT teams is evaluated and the results are very good. For the 142 patients involved in the evaluation, the number of annual days spent in specialist healthcare was reduced by over 8 000 over two years. The evaluation is clear: this approach is good for patients and relatives, and in addition it is good for the economy. I therefore believe that we do a bear service for users if we allow this discussion to be too much about daily places in the field of mental health and health. We see that effect today. We want to counteract that effect. We have abolished municipal co-financing. As early as next year, we will take action to strengthen the interaction on the ready-to-print patients. Today, something is between 3.5 and 7 pst. Of the laydays in mental health protection, patients with ready-to-print conditions, and for multidisciplinary specialised treatment, the figure is between 0.5 and 1 pst. It doesn't sound like a large proportion, but the fact is that many of these patients are ready to print for a very long time. Looking at the group registered in the Norwegian patient register, it looks like patients in mental health protection are on average ready to print for about 60 days. I'm not a professional in this field, but you don't have to have a very good experience to imagine the hopelessness, the lack of power and the frustration that have to build up in patients who have to wait two months to be written out. No one had accepted that a cancer patient or heart surgery had to stay in the hospital for two months after treatment because the municipality did not have the offer they were supposed to have when they finished in the hospital. However, when I do not introduce the obligation to pay in 2017, it is about risk. When our data sources crack as much as they do, we cannot introduce the system without risking a weakening in the supply of patients. If the hospital outflow exceeds the actual number of patients who are ready to print, the hospital supply will be reduced. If the extraction is too small, the municipal offer will be harmful. That is why we need to have better data quality. But this does not mean that we should sit still and see that people live in hospitals for long periods after they have received treatment. Paying for patients who are ready to print does not involve any new responsibility for the municipalities. They already have a crystal clear responsibility for providing the necessary health and care services, including for citizens with mental health challenges and drug dependence. At the same time, many patients who are currently ready to print daily in specialist healthcare will continue to need extensive specialised health care. A more systematic approach, making it easier for the patient to choose treatment in consultation with treatment providers, could provide better and more equivalent treatment for all. ParlaMint-NO_2016-05-26.ana.ud1512e3284	It's been a long day, so I shouldn't go out unnecessarily. It is a consensual committee that supports the setting in this matter. A few brief words about the background: The Ministry has proposed to change the wording of the folklore law § 11-6. As he says today, it is a condition for the right to work clearance that members receive active treatment or participate in labour law measures. The proposition changes the wording from ‘takes active treatment’ to ‘needs active treatment’ and ‘participants in a job-oriented action’ is changed to ‘needs labour-oriented action’. The background is that the right to social security will once again be able to oversee all the assessments made by the Employment and Welfare Act in accordance with Chapter 11 of the Social Security Act, including in this respect, § 11-6. That social security rights should have that competence was the intention of the legislator at the time the schemes were put in place. In other words, social security was not intended and is contrary to what the Storting had as its intention at the time of the introduction of the redundancy fund scheme. This is a follow-up to a 2014 Civilombodsman question, and a majority of hearing authorities have supported the proposed change. It is proposed that the amendment should apply to all cases where the requirement is raised after the amendment takes effect. Thus, the amendment will not change the conditions for the right to work clearance, but will ensure the legal certainty of those who need the scheme, by restoring full testing competence. It's a consensual committee that's behind the vote, and I'm referring to the comments of the bill change in the proposal and to the committee's position.
